#177. Tech outages are causing people to panic-buy. Costco is benefiting, Mizuho says

A series of tech outages has consumers stocking up on emergency supplies.

Costco sales are seeing a lift because of it, according to research firm Mizuho.

In the final week of October, Mizuho estimates, Costco's U.S. comparable sales, excluding gasoline, rose 11% to 12%, up from the normal 6% to 7% the first three weeks in October. Costco reported an 8.1% increase for the month. The firm said the stronger pace is continuing through November, as 21 of the 26 high-ticket generators it tracked on Costco's website sold out as of Sunday night.

Shares of Costco were roughly flat-to-lower in Wednesday morning trading at around $1,107 apiece.

"Consumer anxiety following the Microsoft Outlook outage, the ground stoppage for all U.S. airline flights, and apocalyptic concerns flaring up across social media have all contributed to increased emergency prep sales at COST," Mizuho analyst David Bellinger said in a research note Monday. Bellinger will appear on CNBC's "The Exchange" during the 1 p.m. hour.

On Thursday, an outage occurred in Microsoft's Outlook and Teams, and on Friday the Federal Aviation Administration halted departures nationwide. On Monday, a software outage at JPMorgan Chase caused banking transaction delays, and a system outage delayed United Airlines flights. The National Security Agency said Tuesday that the spy agency's communication network went down temporarily.

The outages appear to come amid a rush to recover software vulnerabilities uncovered by AI tools, and officials are saying there's no reason for panic.

Still, on TikTok and other social media platforms, posts urging people to stock up on food and supplies are gaining significant traction.

Mizuho raised its price target on Costco to $1,150 from $1,100 and kept its buy-equivalent outperform rating. The firm said U.S. comparable sales could grow more than 10% in November.

Mizuho also sees Walmart's Sam's Club benefiting from emergency preparedness sales as shoppers fill up their pantries, and for Home Depot and Lowe's as they buy home improvement emergency supplies.

At Costco, Mizuho will be watching whether the surge brought in new member sign-ups when the warehouse retailer reports its first quarter of fiscal 2027 on Dec. 10.

#175. The engine parts that 'fight the laws of the universe'

A jet engine will have hundreds of turbine blades

Every time you fly, an extraordinary feat of endurance occurs inside the aircraft's engines.

Hidden from view inside the sleek outer nacelle are hundreds of little turbine blades that can shrug off enormous mechanical stresses and temperatures exceeding 1,500C.

It is a true engineering marvel. And these hardy blades are not sculpted or cut from their material â they are grown.

During the 1960s, engineers worked out how to take a molten metal alloy, cast it into the shape of a turbine blade, and then cool it very carefully from one end to the other over a matter of hours. A single crystal or grain of metal alloy forms during this process of gradual, delicate solidification.

Like turning a cup of water into one big snowflake, of your own design, rather than a mini blizzard of many smaller snowflakes.

"It's about as complicated as manufacturing gets," says Ben Fifoot, manufacturing engineer at Rolls-Royce, which makes turbine blades in the UK. "It's a real motivator to get out of bed in the morning."

Only a handful of companies around the world can produce these single crystal turbine blades, and the process has been perfected end-to-end in just five countries, including the US and UK. Engine-makers continually vie for access to the technology.

Rolls-Royce, GE and France's Safran are among the few firms that have perfected the process.

In September, GE Aerospace announced, external it would acquire its turbine blade supplier for $11.75bn (Â£8.78bn). Neither company responded to BBC News' requests for comment but the move has been described as an effort to ensure supply of these critical components.

Separately, in March, Rolls-Royce said it would invest more than Â£19m in its Rotherham blade casting facility.

Turbine blade specialists continue to work on improving the capabilities of this little-known technology. All the while, China is desperate to prove that its engineers can produce turbine blades of the same quality as those in the West. Who will come out on top?

"Demand for [turbine blades] is huge. So few companies have managed to do it, which does give it this kind of magical aura," says Bill Sweetman, an aviation industry writer and consultant.

He suspects GE Aerospace's recent move is about securing reliability and predictability in the notoriously volatile aircraft manufacturing supply chain.

The casts play a crucial part in the cooling process

But why is the single crystal casting process, which has been around for decades now, still so specialist?

To grow one of these blades, asserts Fifoot, is to "fight the laws of the universe".

This is because each molten blade would naturally cool into a solid object consisting of many small grains of metal alloy, all jumbled together. But you don't want a crumbly sugar cube, you want a perfect diamond.

Getting a single crystal to grow into the shape of the blade instead isn't just a matter of cooling the molten version down slowly, the process also uses a bizarre trick called a pigtail.

The pigtail is a small spiral at one end of the blade. Fifoot says that, at the beginning of the cooling process, this spiral "chokes" the flow of solidifying crystals so that just one takes over. A bit like a hairpin corner in an F1 race right before the finish line â one driver can take the lead here and go on to win.

"Sometimes we need to play around with the number of turns or the angles on [the pigtail] to try and get more favourable grain," adds Fifoot. Once the blade is formed, this crucial but sacrificial spiral is cut off.

France's Safran is among the few companies that can make single crystal blades

Blades made by Rolls-Royce are used in aircraft engines including the Trent XWB-84, which powers the Airbus A350-900, and the Trent 1000 XE, for the Boeing 787.

The single crystal is essential to ensure each blade's performance. Sometimes a blade doesn't cool quite as planned, for example two crystals form within it instead of just one, and the metal alloy must be reprocessed so that the manufacturer may re-use the material.

Special ceramic coatings also protect blades from extreme temperatures inside engines. And another important feature is that blades are cast with tiny channels that allow a flow of cooler air to coat them in a kind of film while the aircraft engine is working.

"The drive at the moment [across the industry] is currently to make those cooling passages really intricate," says Paul Withey, at the University of Birmingham, who formerly worked for Rolls-Royce. The more turbulent those little flows of cool air are, he explains, the more they will knock heat out of the surrounding, much hotter air.

And blade-makers also seek to reduce the mass of these components as much as possible.

"If we can take any weight out of it, you can run that engine more efficiently using less fuel â you can spin it faster and hotter," says Fifoot.

The turbine blades are used in the Trent 1000 engine among others

Meanwhile, China hopes to become only the sixth country to master the whole technology chain for single crystal blade casting.

A report in May, external quoted one engineer at the AECC Beijing Institute of Aeronautical Materials claiming that China now had a single crystal alloy "superior to or equivalent to the second-generation single-crystal superalloys widely used in Europe and the US". It costs less to make, too, apparently.

But the West has a "huge advantage" over China given that the majority of commercial airliner production happens there, says Sweetman. Supply chains and the latest innovations are generally clustered in the West. Plus, Western manufacturers also have lengthy safety records under their belts.

China must compete on practically every aspect of aircraft manufacturing, external â by no means just on turbine blades â in order to make serious in-roads. "You can definitely see that happening but it's a long, uphill struggle," adds Sweetman.

Demand for aircraft is currently high, which is already straining various supply chains in the industry. That demand could grow higher still, suggests Withey.

During the next few years, one of the two main aircraft manufacturers, Airbus and Boeing, could launch a new short-haul plane, external, he says. Then, the other would have to follow suit in order to remain competitive.

"There'll be a bun fight," says Withey, over who gets to supply the engines and, by extension, those all-important single crystal turbine blades.

#174. Two new housing reports point to bleak winter ahead, with record low sales

Two new reports on the housing market show activity slowing even more, after a September spike in mortgage rates sent it into a deep freeze.

Pending home sales in October dropped 4.7% month-over-month and were down 16.7% from October of last year, according to the National Association of Realtors. This is the lowest level in the index's 25-year history.

Analysts had been looking for a much smaller decline, likely because mortgage rates pulled back slightly in October, only to rise sharply again last week. The average rate on the 30-year fixed started October at 7.54% according to Mortgage News Daily and ended the month at 7.27%.

The pending count is based on signed contracts — people out shopping and signing deals during the month. It is therefore an indicator of future closed sales.

"Two consecutive months of record-low pending contracts point to very sluggish fourth-quarter home sales closings, and annual existing-home sales could be the lowest since the mid-1990s," said Lawrence Yun, NAR's chief economist in a release.

Regionally, sales were down across the nation with the sharpest drops in the South, which is usually busiest for sales and the West which is most expensive.

Another report released Wednesday showed gains in home construction in October but raised a red flag going forward. Single-family housing starts rose 4% from September, according to the U.S. Census, but were down 3.2% year over year. Building permits, an indicator of future construction, dropped 8.7% for the month and were down 12.9% from October 2025. That is the lowest level for permits since May 2020, the start of the Covid-19 pandemic.

"Builders are dealing with a litany of obstacles to selling homes, whether it's rising costs of land, labor, and materials, high financing costs, or weak buyer demand," wrote Joel Berner, senior economist at Realtor.com. "This month's data shows them slowing down activity at the top of their funnel, which will result in slower months of starts and completions later on."

The construction report follows a bleak reading on homebuilder sentiment, which fell to the lowest level in 14 years in November, on the monthly index from the National Association of Home Builders. Builders cited multiple challenges, including higher mortgage rates, higher costs for energy, materials and labor, as well as weaker consumer confidence.

#173. Robot vessels take on the dangerous work of mine hunting

This South Korean vessel is among the ships damaged in the Strait of Hormuz

Traversing the Strait of Hormuz remains one of the most dangerous trips a vessel can take.

Despite assistance from the US Navy, ships are still being attacked.

"To transit the Strait of Hormuz you need agreement between the captain and the crew to take the risk. You need war risk insurance. And the captain has to make the final decision," says Steve Cameron, a former merchant navy officer and maritime consultant.

Missiles and drones are a threat from the air, but under the surface mines may also be lurking.

"If you're a captain, you'd like to see evidence mines aren't present," Cameron adds.

Modern mines rest on the seabed and detonate when they detect an acoustic or magnetic signal, triggering a pressure wave that will break the keel of a passing vessel.

Others can rise through shallow waters or float, or be powered towards a target.

The technology itself is not difficult or costly, but the mere threat can stop a tanker fleet from sailing.

Ian McFarlane of French defence group Thales is a naval architect who knows mine warfare well. "Mines are a very cheap way of waging a naval war, as the Royal Navy is acutely aware," he says.

Finding sea mines has traditionally been the job of specialist mine-hunting vessels, with crews of around 80 sailors.

To keep humans out of harm's way, an increasing amount of the work is done by systems that are autonomous and monitored from the shore.

They might only need 20 personnel, working in shifts on land. With the recruitment and retention challenges facing Nato navies these highly-portable robot systems are very attractive.

Such tech has been under development for 20 years.

During the 2003 Iraq war the US Navy poked around the seabed looking for mines with a torpedo-shaped robot called the Remus 100.

Pinging around with sonar these little machines operated down to around 100ft depth and spared divers a difficult and dangerous task.

Iran's military published this picture of a US submersible captured in the Gulf

The latest conflict in the Gulf is testing much more capable systems.

The US military is sparing with details about their mine-hunting craft, but we have seen glimpses of them.

Iranian forces hauled an uncrewed US autonomous sub, external out of the waters of the Strait of Hormuz in early September.

The Pentagon claimed this robot, an Extra Large Uncrewed Underwater Vehicle (XLUUV) from US autonomous defence tech venture Anduril, was a malfunctioning older device with no classified equipment on-board.

Whatever its mission, no sailors were imperilled. That, says William Lupton, a naval analyst with Shephard Group, "was a drop in the ocean compared to having prisoners taken", and part of the point of uncrewed vessels.

What if an uncrewed vessel is captured? Nato navies are reluctant to go into details, but computer drives can be wiped or destroyed remotely by various means. This may have taken place inside the humming circuit boards of the XLUUV seized by Iran.

The Thales Uncrewed Surface Vehicle is packed with sensors

Such autonomous tech is being embraced by forces around the world.

Cooperation between Thales, the Royal Navy, and France's Marine Nationale has produced a robot motor launch.

The Thales Uncrewed Surface Vehicle (USV) is packed with sensors programmed to detect mines via sonar that was developed for the oil and gas industries.

It tows a 5m long rectangular box, similar to a large torpedo, that takes a hi-resolution look at the seabed and creates a 3D picture refined by AI. The latest mines are designed to blend into their surroundings and the software spots anything that looks like a mine.

Autonomous systems are cheaper than conventional minehunters, plus they can be deployed, along with containerised control centres, by air.

Steve Prest, associate fellow at the RUSI defence think tank, introduced uncrewed mine hunting into the Royal Navy and was its first fleet robotics officer, a title he acquired in 2014.

"There is a bunch of ways to look for a mine using robot boats or robot subs, and while you can't prove the negative, that no mines are there, you can reduce the risk."

He says US robot minehunters can either blow up a mine or just mark the spot as somewhere to avoid.

"We'll see more use of autonomy but also the missions and capability will expand. And AI will be analysing data from those vessels on the spot as opposed to data being analysed when a drone returns."

The Royal Navy recently announced its concept of a hybrid navy - mixing drones with conventional warships. But Prest thinks for this vision to succeed the fleet must operate as one distributed force, sharing data in what he terms a cognitive architecture.

The robot only makes a real difference as part of this scheme and it's a mistake to focus on individual uncrewed assets. "Uncrewed or autonomous vessels are not the operating system. They are applications running on it."

Steve Prest introduced uncrewed mine hunting into the Royal Navy

While robots are on the rise, people are what really matter in the Strait.

Neil Roberts speaks for insurers at Lloyd's of London on marine matters and says the psychological impact of the mine threat is inescapable, as is the uncertainty around just how many mines, if any, are out there.

"The US says it has cleared the Omani route and believes the mine threat is being managed, with shipments running at about 75% of pre-conflict levels."

Fear of the unknown is the most potent aspect of the sea mine threat and robots are now the weapon of choice to confront that fear.

#169. The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

BBC's Samira Hussain reports from Wall Street on what the fall of the tech superstar means.

Jensen Huang, known for sporting a black leather jacket to shareholder events, appeared to have it all. He was courted by leaders in the US and China alike, worshipped by investors and seen as a loyal family man. But a rockstar approach to his personal life has ultimately led to his resignation.

The 63-year-old has been married since 1985, long before the foundation of Nvidia, the company that propelled him to tech stardom.

He is known as a workaholic and a demanding manager, with high standards for those who worked for him, which included his own children, promoted within the company. But he has seen his personal reputation go into freefall this week, undone by romantic entanglements that throw doubt on his focus and his judgement.

On Thursday, he resigned following back-to-back revelations of two affairs. News of the first - a relationship with a businesswoman alleged to be a Chinese spy - ricocheted around the world, raising eyebrows among investors. It was followed on Wednesday by reports that the tech boss had also had an affair with an Nvidia colleague.

His departure creates uncertainty in the global stock market, which was already knocked-off balance in recent weeks. Nvidia is a linchpin in the US's race for technology leadership with China.

Huang may not be a household name on the same level as Elon Musk or Mark Zuckerberg.

But his role in the tech industry, especially in relation to the explosion of artificial intelligence in the last three years, may prove more consequential. Nvidia dominates the market for the microchips that are used to power the new technology.

The company, which is still valued at nearly $4n (Â£3.02tn) despite recent events, is at the centre of the AI race between the US and China, the world's tech and economic powerhouses.

Huang enjoyed access to the seats of political power on an unrivalled scale, being courted by both US President Donald Trump and China's President Xi Jinping.

His remarkable rise to the top of his profession has led to him being dubbed the "Taylor Swift of tech" and seen him amass an estimated fortune of $145bn, according to the Bloomberg Billionaires Index.

Despite always donning his trademark all-black outfits and taking a playful approach to investor presentations, Huang has worked for years to present himself as a family man, someone who exemplified the American Dream.

Born in Taiwan in 1963, Huang and his family moved to the west coast of the US when he was nine years old. He graduated as an electrical engineer from Oregon State University, and then with a Master's degree in the same field from Stanford University.

It was at college where he met his wife, Lori Mills. The pair were lab partners and married in 1985, eight years before Huang founded Nvidia.

They have two children, Madison and Spencer, who now both work in senior roles at Nvidia.

The company became a key provider of the computer chips and circuits used for gaming before it expanded in a new direction. It now creates the key processing chips used to power the growing AI technology.

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigns after tech giant rocked by second affair allegation

Who could replace Jensen Huang as Nvidia's chief executive?

Huang has hailed AI as a "new industrial revolution" that is on par with the advent of electricity. Nvidia is the world's second most valuable company and its customers include the biggest firms on the planet, Google, Meta, Amazon, Microsoft, SpaceX, Tesla, OpenAI and Anthropic, all spending big on its products.

So it is no surprise that at a recent AI summit at the White House, Huang was picked out from a group of 42 to flank the president, alongside Elon Musk, both men with whom Trump has famously close relationships.

"Jensen Huang is a tech visionary of the likes of Steve Jobs," Stephen Witt, the author of Thinking Machine, a biography on Jensen Huang, told the BBC, referring to the late boss of Apple. He added that Huang was responsible for creating AI hardware as we know it today.

He described him as a "demanding" boss, who "really pushes his people to the limit".

But while the former Nvidia CEO is "very brilliant" and a "world-class computer scientist", there's also "a level of arrogance" and "a level of self-deception as well", he said.

Huang had gone to great lengths to present himself as a family man, he added, something that was "very important to him".

"He praises his wife, he talks about her in very glowing ways," Witt added. "The fallout of this would be bad for anyone's family. But for someone who has gone to such great lengths to cultivate an image of being a family guy and who keeps his children very close, it is very bad."

First reports emerged on 29 October in the New York Times that Huang had conducted a relationship with Alice Huang, no relation. US officials allege the businesswoman associated with Singapore-based firm Megaspeed is a Chinese intelligence asset.

After the revelations of the first affair, Huang admitted to having been "unfaithful" to his wife and apologised for hurting their family.

Trump, as well as other leaders in the AI tech industry, initially backed Huang to continue in his role at Nvidia. The president described him as "honourable".

But the reporting of a second affair, with Kimberly Powell, vice president of healthcare who has worked at Nvidia for 18 years, prompted a board meeting, followed by the announcement Huang was stepping down.

In the aftermath of that news Trump said Huang had done "a fantastic job" and proposed Tim Cook of Apple as a suitable replacement at the head of Nvidia.

#166. How a sex scandal at Nvidia is putting pressure on the White House

How could the Nvidia CEOâs affair threaten US national security?

How close is Donald Trump to Jensen Huang?

The Nvidia boss was one of the few business leaders with direct access to the US president. He had met Trump repeatedly, travelled with him to Britain and, in May, joined him on Air Force One for his trip to China.

Jensen Huang wasn't just another tech billionaire seeking the president's ear. He had become an important voice in one of the biggest arguments inside the administration: how much of America's most advanced technology should be allowed to reach China.

Nvidia makes the chips that power the AI revolution. Washington has restricted exports of its most advanced processors to China since 2022, believing they could help Beijing develop its military and technological capabilities.

But enforcing those restrictions is difficult. Chips can be bought legally by customers in countries such as Singapore and Malaysia and then diverted to China. Chinese companies can also rent access to computing power through data centres outside the country.

Jensen Huang has long argued that the restrictions are counterproductive. They shut Nvidia out of a huge market and encourage Chinese companies to develop their own alternatives. Trump appeared sympathetic to this view, allowing Nvidia to resume sales of certain chips to China last year in return for a share of the revenue, although its most advanced Blackwell chips remain off limits.

But that relationship now looks rather more complicated.

The allegations surrounding Jensen Huang's reported affair with Alice Huang, of no relation but a former executive at Singapore-based company Megaspeed, have raised the possibility that a foreign intelligence operation was targeting not just a powerful businessman, but someone with unusually close access to the American president.

US intelligence reportedly suspects Alice Huang of having links to Chinese intelligence. Beijing has rejected the allegations. Nvidia's board says it has no reason to believe company secrets or national security were compromised. But the affair has opened up a much bigger question: what did the administration in Washington know, and what did it do about it?

That question leads directly to Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick.

His department was responsible for controlling exports of sensitive American technology. It was also being pressed to act on concerns about Megaspeed, an Nvidia customer suspected of helping divert advanced chips to China.

On Thursday, Megaspeed chief executive, Tan Yong Pong, also known as James Tan, said the company "takes its compliance obligations under US export control laws extremely seriously" and is "committed to working constructively and transparently with the US government". He said the company had co-operated with US Commerce Department inspections.

A highly classified memo was shared with Lutnick about the concerns surrounding Alice Huang and Megaspeed. He resisted taking action against the company, a decision that is now under scrutiny.

Other senior officials wanted a tougher response.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent was among those who reportedly questioned why Megaspeed had not been placed on the Commerce Department's blacklist.

On Friday, Bessent pointed the finger at Commerce for not blacklisting the company, telling CNBC that several government agencies "were in favour of putting Megaspeed on the entity list for months".

"But Commerce has the pen... and you will have to ask them about their internal process," he added.

There have also been questions about why an investigation into the Singapore-based company was stopped after Nvidia intervened. The New York Times reported that Nvidia's general counsel contacted Commerce Department investigators probing suspected chip smuggling by Megaspeed invoking a senior Trump appointee. The department allegedly then froze its investigation and handed the case to the Justice Department.

This apparently highlighted a significant disagreement about the direction of Trump's China policy. Huang wanted access to the Chinese market. Other officials were supposedly worried about the national security consequences. Lutnick was caught in the middle.

Ex-Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang summoned to testify before Congress about affair with alleged Chinese spy

Bessent blames Lutnick's Commerce Department for failing to block Megaspeed

Members of Congress from both parties want answers. Democrats have launched an investigation into how the Commerce Department vetted Chinese companies approved to buy AI chips. Republicans are also questioning the administration's approach.

And Jensen Huang's departure has not eased the pressure.

Republican representative John Moolenaar, who chairs the House Select Committee on China, said it was in the national interest for Nvidia and other American AI firms to succeed, but that companies "should never jeopardise our nation's security" by giving Chinese firms access to their most advanced chips.

Senator Elizabeth Warren, the top Democrat on the Senate Banking Committee, said Huang's resignation did not absolve him of responsibility, and urged Lutnick to explain why export control loopholes remain open.

Meanwhile Jensen Huang's team told Republican lawmakers on Wednesday that he plans to cooperate with their request to testify behind closed doors.

The White House has not responded to the BBC's request for comment.

It has, however, told the New York Post that Trump has complete confidence in Lutnick, describing him as "the most transformative commerce secretary in modern history".

That is a fairly emphatic show of support, particularly amid reports that officials are weighing whether Lutnick should be forced out.

There are some obvious questions. If Lutnick had indeed been warned about the risks, why did his department fail to act? And if other senior officials were indeed pressing for action, why did it take so long?

The BBC has contacted the Commerce Department for comment. But there is an even bigger question for Trump.

The president cultivated a close relationship with Huang. He was directly involved in decisions about chip sales to China. And, according to reporting in the New York Times, he was briefed on the intelligence concerns.

This is becoming difficult to dismiss as the misjudgement of one businessman or one government department.

Jensen Huang's personal life may have triggered the crisis, but the questions now reach into the White House itself. How much influence did he have? What did the president know? And who was ultimately responsible for protecting America's most sensitive technology?

Who is Nvidia's interim CEO Colette Kress - and who could take over permanently?

The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

#162. Where are the most jobs for 18-24 year-olds?

If you're one of the nearly one million people aged 16-24 not in education, employment or training - otherwise known as "Neet" - then you might be wondering where to start.

Under-25s should be guaranteed job placements or training to help them find permanent work, a major review aimed at cutting the number of Neets will say.

But how many jobs are there where you live? Use our look-up tool to find out.

Burnham wants Neets report to be watershed moment, No 10 says, as Tories criticise welfare and tax proposals

Where you live will often have a big impact on your ability to find a job.

The Institute for Public Policy Research (IPPR) think tank used online job adverts to help identify the number of vacancies suitable for young people in the area they live, relative to the size of the local population.

It used AI to help identify the jobs, which were broadly defined as roles requiring relatively low experience or education.

According to its analysis, South Oxfordshire had 387 suitable vacancies per 1,000 18 to 24-year-olds in the area - the most of any local authority. Rotherham had the least, at 53 per 1,000.

Use this tool to check out the situation in your area. The data for London is only available for the region as a whole and is not broken down by individual councils.

Some people live in one area and work in another, but the analysis did not account for this.

Even if there are jobs available in your area, you might not find one in the sector you want to work in.

The sector with the highest average number of entry-level vacancies in the year to October was teaching, with most of those jobs being for teaching assistants, learning support and cover supervisor roles, according to jobs website Adzuna.

The next largest was hospitality and catering, traditionally an area where younger people get their first experience of work.

Adzuna, a major online jobs website, includes adverts for full-time roles marked as entry-level, those asking for up to two years' experience, graduate roles, internships and apprenticeships.

Accounting and finance had the third-highest number of job openings.

Figures on salaries for vacancies can be patchy because many employers do not include them in job adverts.

Prime Minister Andy Burnham recently urged businesses to start publishing salaries ahead of plans to make pay details compulsory in adverts.

Only about 40% of Adzuna's jobs data for the year to October stated a salary, although that figure varies widely by sector.

Here are the average salaries for jobs in the sectors with the largest number of entry-level jobs, according to Adzuna. The highest average salary is Â£30,530 in engineering, while social care has the lowest at Â£25,880.

While it can be hard for anyone looking for their first job, the situation is also tough for older jobseekers.

Across the whole economy there were 690,000 jobs available in the August to October period this year, which is the lowest number since early 2021.

The Covid pandemic led to huge swings in the number of job vacancies available as companies reacted to shutdowns and then reopened as restrictions were lifted.

Stripping out the fluctuations seen during the pandemic, the number of vacancies is now at its lowest level for 12 years.

Why Milburn's Neets reforms will only save Â£200m a year by 2036

Apprenticeships have often been a way for people to get their first taste of employment.

In England the number of people starting apprenticeships has risen in recent years, although this has been driven by an increase in those aged 25 and over after schemes for older workers were fully rolled out in 2008.

The number of people aged 24 and under starting apprenticeships in England in 2025 was 172,230, down from a peak of 291,300 in 2012.

#160. Republicans outspent Democrats for first time in over a decade in record 2026 midterm election political ad blitz

Political advertisers spent a record $12.06 billion during the 2025-26 elections, making it the first time a midterm cycle outspent a presidential cycle, according to a report from advertising intelligence company AdImpact released Tuesday.

Total spending exceeded AdImpact's June projection by more than $460 million.

The elections also marked the first time in over a decade that Republicans have outspent Democrats on advertisements, according to AdImpact.

Despite Republicans leading spending, John Link, senior vice president of data at AdImpact, said that "Dems still led or were generally even with overall airings and impressions."

The record spending extended across the board, with every type of race reaching a new high. Senate spending hit $3.39 billion, while spending on gubernatorial elections reached $2.61 billion, spending on races in the House of Representatives grew to $2.44 billion and state legislative races totaled $602 million, all records, according to AdImpact.

The 2026 midterm cycle was largely powered by megadonors, with SpaceX and Tesla CEO Elon Musk becoming the largest contributor by funneling more than $366 million into campaigns.

The biggest donors in this year's election disproportionately favored Republicans, according to a CNBC analysis of Federal Election Commission records. Fourteen out of the 20 largest donor groups supported GOP candidates, according to that analysis.

Republicans spent $5.51 billion on political advertisements, compared with $5.11 billion spent by Democrats, according to AdImpact.

While Republican spending increased by 27% from 2024, spending by Democrats fell by 4%.

Fundraisers told CNBC in interviews that Democratic donors' hesitancy largely stems from memories of Kamala Harris' defeat in 2024, unease about the rise of democratic socialism and distrust in party leadership.

Though they were outspent, Democrats made sweeping gains against Republicans in races nationwide, taking control of both the House and the Senate and flipping seats in some of the most competitive places, including Texas and Kansas.

It is not the first time spending did not align with the winners. In 2024, President Donald Trump defeated former Vice President Harris despite Democrats outspending Republicans.

While party majorities in Congress have switched hands, the most popular medium for political ads has not changed.

Broadcast television represented the largest category for spending in this year's election, accounting for 49%, or $5.85 billion, of political ad spending, according to AdImpact. Broadcast's share of political ad dollars is up slightly from 48% in 2024.

Broadcast TV's strength comes despite facing pressure from falling viewership and increasing competition from streaming.

Industry experts have told CNBC that political advertisers continue to spend on broadcast because it reaches local markets and active voting populations. The Associated Press also reported that airtime was so expensive during the midterm elections that some candidates, including Republican Ken Paxton in Texas, bought ads during national sporting events, figuring that reaching enough constituents through a national broadcast might actually be less expensive than buying across the state's local television markets.

The fastest-growing categories in this year's cycle were connected TV, or internet-connected devices like Apple TV or Roku, with $2.66 billion, and digital, with $1.91 billion. Both mediums grew double digits from the 2024 presidential election. Spending on cable and radio declined 7% and 13%, respectively, from 2024.

#158. As Trump and Xi meet, China's AI dreams face an unexpected hurdle: the Nvidia spy scandal

Jensen Huang is out but his shadow looms over the upcoming meeting between US President Donald Trump and his Chinese counterpart Xi Jinping this week.

For Beijing, the former Nvidia CEO's dramatic fall is a major challenge - and not just because of claims that a Chinese spy may have honey-trapped Huang, one of the artificial intelligence industry's most influential figures.

Rather, the allegations surrounding Huang and his affair with Chinese businesswoman Alice Huang are turning into a new flashpoint in an already fragile US-China relationship.

US intelligence reports claim she had ties to Beijing's intelligence apparatus - in particular the Ministry of State Security, China's main civilian spy agency.

The claim remains unproven, but it has already reignited debate in Washington over exports of advanced chips to China and whether the Trump administration has been too willing to ease restrictions.

For years now, advanced computer chips have been a key battleground in the US-China rivalry because they are essential for AI, supercomputing and military technology.

Chinese officials are worried this scandal could strengthen voices in Washington who argue in favour of further limiting Chinese access to cutting-edge American tech.

Just this week, the Trump administration introduced new export restrictions on Chinese firms that access advanced US chips in third countries such as Malaysia, Thailand or Brazil. They typically do this by renting computing power in data centres abroad to train their AI models. But some of the firms, like the one Alice Huang worked for, are also accused of smuggling chips to China, although they have denied this.

Until now, this controversy appeared to be a sideshow in US-China relations. But with Beijing calling the new rules "economic coercion" on the eve of Trump arriving in Shenzhen, there is a growing risk it could become something much bigger.

Watch: Jensen Huang speaks to the BBC's Faisal Islam in 2025

Democrats, who will control Congress come January, have made their position clear. Last Wednesday top Democrat Ted Lieu told Politico the Trump administration had been "1,000%" too deferential to Nvidia, putting national security at risk, while fellow Democrat Zoe Lofgren suggested Congress would now be more likely to restrict sales of AI chips to China.

The BBC has approached the White House for comment.

Beijing's foreign ministry had described the spy claims as a "smear against China".

"The US should stop fabricating so-called espionage narratives to find pretexts for imposing technological blockades," spokesperson Mao Ning said last week.

The ministry also seemed keen to keep the meeting on track, saying the two countries had kept "close communication regarding interactions between the two heads of state".

For the last year, Trump and Xi have been seeking to stabilise relations after a damaging tariff war that almost brought trade between the two countries to a standstill.

The Trump administration's decision to permit sales of advanced Nvidia H200 chips to China in late 2025 was seen as evidence that both sides were searching for areas of co-operation, even while competition intensified.

It was a rare move given Washington had spent years trying to limit China's access to the most advanced chips.

Many in the US have long viewed these chips as a national security issue, arguing they could strengthen China's military and surveillance capabilities. Jensen Huang, who rejects that claim, advocated lifting bans on the export of high-performing chips to China. Beijing argues the restrictions are an attempt to contain its rise.

History has taught Beijing and Washington that spy scandals rarely remain confined to the intelligence world.

Analysts in China already see echoes of an incident in 2023 when a suspected surveillance balloon drifted through the skies over the US before an American fighter jet shot it down. Beijing insisted it was a civilian meteorological airship that had strayed off course.

The suspected Chinese spy balloon was shot down days after it was spotted over the US

But a planned trip by then US Secretary of State Antony Blinken was cancelled and communication between the two governments deteriorated sharply.

The incident showed how quickly a security dispute could overwhelm diplomatic efforts and push relations into a deep freeze.

"The mere passage of a balloon sent American public opinion and politicians into a state of high alert, as if a "Chinese threat" were looming directly overhead," said the state media outlet, the Global Times this week as it made a direct comparison with the Nvidia scandal.

"No-one on the US side has ever apologised or taken responsibility for the genuine damage this caused to US-China relations," it added.

The state-run outlet also questioned why the Nvidia story had emerged so soon after "the hard-won detente" between Beijing and Washington, following a meeting between the two countries' leaders in September.

"Is someone behind the scenes pushing an agenda and stirring up confrontation?" it asked.

Both Trump and Xi have significant incentives to keep talking.

China's economy remains under pressure and is increasingly reliant on exports. Xi needs trade to keep flowing and this meeting is a key stepping stone to replacing the current trade truce that is due to expire in January.

Meanwhile Trump has repeatedly touted his close relationship with Xi and argued that maintaining dialogue is essential to managing competition between the two powers.

The US president is in his last two years and faces an uphill struggle to pass domestic legislation after the midterms. To counter this, he could be looking for some foreign policy wins.

He may also hope for Xi's help to end the war in the Middle East.

Neither side will want to be discussing intelligence concerns and technology controls instead of trade, investment and economic co-operation.

The summit is also being carefully choreographed by Beijing to show off its technological prowess in Shenzhen - a deeply symbolic choice of city.

It's home to some of the country's biggest tech companies and has become a showcase for Beijing's ambitions in AI and advanced manufacturing.

Also, given Washington's tighter restrictions on advanced chips, Beijing has options for retaliation.

One of its most powerful tools is its dominance of key rare earth minerals. China processes much of the world's supply for materials that are used in everything from electric vehicles and smartphones to military equipment.

Analysts have long warned that technological restrictions can trigger a cycle of retaliation in which each side reaches for its strongest economic leverage.

That would shift the current trend of US-China relations from engagement back towards mutual suspicion.

#155. Read Jim Cramer’s full interview with Nvidia’s Colette Kress: Demand, buybacks and China

Nvidia delivered another beat-and-raise quarter on Tuesday in the chipmaker's first report since Jensen Huang abruptly resigned as CEO last week amid a widening affair scandal. Revenue more than doubled from a year earlier, exceeding estimates by nearly $7 billion, while earnings topped expectations by 20 cents per share. An excellent quarter by almost any measure.

But investors remain skeptical about Nvidia's future without Huang, who famously founded the company at a San Jose Denny's in 1993. Shares fell 2.5% Wednesday, despite opening higher on the session. Will the company lose its smartest executives and engineers? Would Nvidia remain banker to the increasingly shaky neoclouds? Can it stay ahead of the competition?

On Tuesday night, Colette Kress sat down with CNBC's Jim Cramer shortly after delivering her first earnings call as interim CEO to answer these pressing questions and more.

This transcript has been edited for clarity.

Jim Cramer: After the bell, Nvidia reported earnings, and what a quarter it was. The stock jumped in after hours in response. This is the first time we are hearing from the company since its founder, Jensen Huang, stepped down as CEO last week. So let's check in with Colette Kress, Nvidia's longtime chief financial officer and now its interim CEO. Ms. Kress, welcome to "Mad Money."

Colette Kress: Thank you for having me, Jim. It's really great to see you.

Cramer: Thank you, Colette. The most important thing I heard tonight was that you increased your revenue growth forecast for next year. Previously, you said you would grow 70%. Now you're saying 75%, off of a higher base. How are you able to do that?

Kress: Thanks. Very, very important to understand how much our customers are interested in our compute infrastructure and how much they need the agentic solutions out there. So the most important thing that we have been seeing is just, again, a continuous growth in terms of the demand that's out there. But we also have been working in terms of our supply. So an increase in supply, working with all of our different suppliers, whether that be focusing in terms of what we need in terms of chips, what we need in terms of advanced packaging or memory, all of that is also coming together. We felt very confident, therefore, that we could establish that we will grow 75% year over year for fiscal year '28.

Cramer: Now, a lot of people have been saying that there was one man at this company. I've talked with you. There are another 49,000. How is the team holding up? Are there people who are saying, "You know what, this is not the Nvidia I know, I don't want to be there?" Is there a brain drain, as some people have told me?

Kress: Yeah, really great question. And remember, the company has been built on the perspective that employees see Nvidia as their life's work. And many of them have been here for decades, working with us from both a technology and a business-and-operations perspective. And now where we're standing is focusing on the business, focusing on getting all of that work done. And I had been working already with leadership. Leadership is supportive of everything we're doing and is standing up to help as we pull all employees and leaders together to move forward. But we have high confidence that our employees are definitely still working, still working with us, and enjoy really the work here at Nvidia.

Cramer: I have talked to you many times about the idea that the cheapest stock in the world is yours, and that's why you don't necessarily have to help other people buy your chips when you can just go buy your own stock. Where are you on buybacks versus lending aggressive amounts of money to, say, neoclouds? Because I think that the buybacks are a lot better for shareholders.

Kress: We have now been looking at the capability of making significant stock repurchases for our company. That is enabled by our significant growth and the cash we will have. During the quarter, we discussed increasing the amount of additional stock repurchases to more than $150 billion. That adds to a total amount at this point of $277 billion that we're going to be able to provide. We are completely committed to that. We do believe that that's something we're going to be able to complete all the way through, just to the end of 2027. So we are in line and continuing our stock repurchases for sure.

Cramer: OK. Thank you. That's important. And it's more aggressive than I thought. And I'm glad to hear that. Now, why are there many different reports about your company, and even about your accounting? I know you as the person who is in charge of the numbers. For instance, I read two different analysts who say you may be on the hook for about $530 billion in guarantees, which is worrisome because it could have a huge impact on your cash flow.  I heard a new disclosure tonight that made me feel like the on-the-hook number is not true.

Kress: Right now, our guarantees have been thoughtfully completed to date. And our guarantees are approximately about $118 billion, which includes one of the most important ones: a land, power, and shell backstop that we have with Portsmouth here in the United States, the best site for what we see, the future of multiple, multiple buildings working together to do that, about 4.5 gigawatts to do so. That is solely for Nvidia's infrastructure. It's working very well and is getting ready for all the builds it needs to do. And that is the most significant part of our guarantees. We will continue to do the best due diligence and thought regarding any additional guarantees that need to be done. That means we're going to be working with companies to make sure we understand where they are in terms of their land, power, shell builds, how they are doing in terms of getting ready for the compute, getting the capital that they need, and making sure that they have the end customer, the off-take that we'll need to complete that. So it's going to be a top priority. But we are all working together to make sure that we can support the most important ones that we think are running very well, and that's going to be a top priority for us.

Cramer: Speaking of priorities, from a lot of reporting, again, because I want to try to get all this stuff out. You were kind enough to come on and speak about anything unrehearsed. Based on much of the reporting over the last two weeks, it seems that the aggressive investments in the ecosystem, including some companies that are less than investment-grade, were a priority for your predecessor and his family members. I want to know if you were pushing back on those? And should we see less and expect less support for the ecosystem and more for the shareholders?

Kress: Our investments are an important part of the work we can do, focusing on a set of organizations that may be AI-native, maybe new types of companies starting, as well as helping those that want to build new types of models in the market. These are some of the areas that we focused on. But, yes, we're very disciplined in terms of how we think that through. There's a lot of discussion internally with a lot of the teams on what we think are the best ones that we want to do. And we're going to continue down that path to make sure we choose the right investments for us to work through going forward. But most importantly, these are companies in areas where we can see additional growth in AI. And I think those are important for us. But, again, stepping back to our business and how important our capital is going to be for procuring supply and getting ready for all of our employees as well, and all the work that we need to do from the engineering, that's going to be our top priority in terms of our use of capital to do so. So that's what I think is the most important.

Cramer: You are in a very competitive situation. For instance, AMD has a product that's a GPU that is much touted, and they think it can be moved in quicker. I know that some of the hyperscalers are very good at developing their own chips, which makes some people say, "Wait a second. Is what's really happening that Nvidia is supporting some companies that may not be, let's say, investment-grade or the kind of customer you would want — neoclouds — because it's losing business from the traditional hyperscalers and their shares are going down. Is that true?

Kress: Interesting statement, but no, it is not true. If we want to think about where we are with our hyperscalers, they are right now feverishly looking for more focus on the compute we have available. I have seen already with many of these contacting me and saying, " Great to see everything intact. Great to see that there's more supply. I need more demand." So they are all with us. And remember, there's a reason why. That is an amazing type of architecture that has been built through so much work with the different types of engineers. It is the most durable, fungible, and performant type of infrastructure out there. The idea that anything is changing among our customer types is just not true. They are all still with us. And we have a very deep breadth of different customers. And I think that's important to understand. These will be needed for all the different organizations overseas as well. They're going to want to say, "How do I have someone building a different type of data center overseas that they would want to see as well?" So I do believe it is appropriate to have different types of organizations that will be using this type of compute. But keep in mind, the stock, the amount of different types of customers, is broad. And we are going to continue to focus on that.

Cramer: This level of demand makes me ask you a particular question, which is that, if you were no longer allowed or wanted to ship into Southeast Asia for fear that it might end up in China, the flow back would not overwhelm pricing and drive it down, but instead would just help sate some of demand, but not all the demand? You do not need Southeast Asia to make or advance your numbers?

Kress: Well, what we are always working on is: Where do we feel we can provide the demand to our customers? We are always very, very cautious in terms of where we will be shipping to and making sure, yes, the data center is ready, and whether or not we also know that off-take and we know that customer piece of it. That is part of our process, and we will continue there. Let's just remember what we are right now in terms of our shipments to China. Our shipments to China are approximately about 10%. But that is our gaming business. That is our automotive business. That is our workstation business that is going there. As you also saw us announce, we would only have the licensed H200s [Nvidia's data center GPUs], and those went to China within this last quarter. And we are no longer shipping H200 at all, let alone not shipping it to China. So we are very careful and will continue to focus on that.

Cramer: I do want to ask you something that I think was a question that was handled by the board, but I didn't necessarily get an answer that I felt satisfactory. I understand, and have heard, that the chairman of the board says the board does not intend to ask Jensen Huang to return as CEO. But I have to say that perhaps the board would consider, after a thorough investigation — which I do not believe they did, frankly — that Jensen's value to our nation's technology, as expressed by our president today, could lead him to do something else for you. Perhaps a chief technologist or an ambassador for Nvidia's dominance who would not run afoul of either the SEC or anything done by Commerce or the Secretary of the Treasury.

Kress: Jensen resigned from his position with Nvidia, and the board accepted his resignation. The board has determined that they will not reinstate Jensen. And that is our statement and what the board has decided to do. We want to think about growing our business right now. We want to grow in everything we do with our employees, our customers, and our shareholders, and that is where we will focus moving forward.

Cramer: Do you think that it's possible that the two-way compute market project that Jensen started, that was going to be done with Goldman, with BlackRock, with Apollo, will continue? That's $500 billion in financing to make it happen the way we think it should.

Kress: Those financing funds are very important to us. We announced that they will continue to work with us, and their continued assistance with many of these companies that may be just shy of investment-grade and want to explore financing options; we're excited that they are part of this work. They are part of helping us create an asset class of our systems as well. No other company has that durability and fungibility outside of Nvidia. So they are important partners. We're going to continue working to help many of the different companies get the financing that they may want to do and the efficiency of that financing.

Cramer: You cleared up many questions for us tonight, and I really appreciate it. I know there are a lot of people who, I think, say a lot of things about your company that aren't true. I want to thank you, Colette Kress, who's been true north for me for many, many years, for as long as we've known each other. I want to say — I would say congratulations on the interim CEO. I'm glad you were on our show tonight, and I hope those who want to understand what kind of company this really is go back over what she said. It's quite different from what I hear every day on Wall Street. Colette Kress is the former CFO, and interim CEO, and it was great to have you on the show. Thank you, Colette.

#154. Waymo pulls driverless taxis from all US freeways

Waymo has temporarily suspended its driverless vehicles from operating on all US freeways, the company has said.

The decision comes after California banned Waymo's driverless taxis from state freeways following a series of recent traffic incidents.

California's Department of Motor Vehicles (DMV) cited an "imminent hazard to public safety" for the ban, noting an incident this month in which several driverless Waymo vehicles entered an active construction zone on US Route 101.

Waymo said it was working with state officials "diligently and responsibly to resume freeway operations", adding that no injuries or collisions were reported following the incident on US Route 101 south of San Francisco.

California's DMV said Waymo's driverless taxis presented a risk "due to recurring incursions by its vehicles into active construction zones where construction workers, vehicles and equipment are present".

In another incident earlier this month, footage widely seen on social media appeared to show a Waymo car trying to overtake patrol vehicles that were deliberately slowing the pace of traffic on a San Francisco highway.

The California ban, which came into effect on 11 November, would remain in place until Waymo "demonstrates to the department that it has taken effective corrective actions to address the deficiencies that precipitated the restrictions".

A Waymo spokesperson said: "In alignment with our safety-first principles, we have also temporarily restricted freeway operations across our other operating markets to evaluate our performance."

Data on Waymo's website suggests that its cars, compared to human drivers, are involved in 95% fewer crashes resulting in serious injury.

Waymo currently operates driverless taxis in 15 US cities, but only offered freeway rides in a handful of those locations, including San Francisco, Los Angeles and Miami.

The company's driverless taxis can continue to operate on regular city streets.

In May, Waymo recalled thousands of its self-driving cars in the US over a software issue that could allow vehicles to drive into flooded roads.

In the same month, multiple Waymo cars were spotted driving around a cul-de-sac in Atlanta, Georgia, in the early hours of the morning.

#152. U.S. officially loses measles elimination status amid ongoing outbreaks

The U.S. on Tuesday lost its official designation as a measles-free nation, more than 25 years after it eliminated the highly contagious virus.

The Pan American Health Organization announced the decision, which came after an independent panel of eight scientific experts convened last week to consider the country's epidemiological evidence. The director of PAHO, the regional branch of the World Health Organization, made the final call that measles is endemic — or always present — in the U.S. following 12 months of continuous transmission.

In other words, measles is spreading regularly in the country again, with ongoing outbreaks sparking new ones.

"The commission concluded that endemic measles transmission has now been reestablished in Bolivia, Mexico and the United States, and recommended that their measles elimination status be withdrawn — a recommendation which I have accepted," Dr. Jarbas Barbosa, PAHO director, said during a news briefing on Tuesday.

It's a devastating step backward for American public health, as declining childhood vaccination rates and vaccine hesitancy fuel outbreaks across the country that have sickened thousands of people and led to a tragic string of preventable deaths. Among the deaths where vaccination status was reported, all were unvaccinated.

More than 5,400 measles cases had been reported nationwide in 2026 as of mid-November, marking the country's worst outbreak in more than three decades. States have confirmed 10 deaths associated with the disease so far in 2026: seven in Pennsylvania, one in New York and two in Wisconsin.

The commission found that out of 52 U.S. states and territories it tracked, 48 experienced measles outbreaks during the reporting period between January 2025 and January 2026, PAHO Assistant Director Dr. Rhonda Sealey-Thomas said during the briefing. But Barbosa said the countries are already taking action to control outbreaks, such as strengthening vaccination, surveillance for timely case detection and outbreak response efforts.

The U.S. in particular has ramped up vaccination efforts to close immunity gaps, particularly in areas with low immunization coverage experiencing outbreaks, said Dr. Daniel Salas, executive manager of PAHO's Special Program for Comprehensive Immunization. He said PAHO expects the U.S. to present its corrective action plan in the coming weeks, outlining strategies to regain its designation.

In a Tuesday release, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention said it will continue to ensure all communities have the resources to prevent and respond to outbreaks and is committed to working with states and other local partners to restore the U.S.'s measles elimination status.

CDC Director Dr. Erica Schwartz said in the release that the measles elimination designation "is not what protects Americans. Vaccination does." She said the agency has invested $100 million in state and local health departments to reach communities where transmission is occurring.

Last week, Schwartz told Reuters that the agency is going to be "doing the same exact thing that we've been doing today" if the U.S. loses its measles-free status.

The decision also poses a new test for the Trump administration and its Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr., who has taken steps to change recommendations for the childhood measles, mumps and rubella, or MMR, vaccine and has a long history of questioning its safety without evidence.

But Kennedy, in a Tuesday X post, said he continues to recommend MMR vaccination as "the most effective way to prevent measles."

"We are working to close vaccination gaps and rebuild public trust," Kennedy said. "We will answer families' questions honestly about vaccine benefits and risks and work with trusted health care providers and community leaders to prevent measles, limit its spread, and support timely treatment."

CNBC previously reported on how the return of measles in the U.S. exposes weaknesses in the nation's public health system that raise concerns about the return of other eliminated diseases. The resurgence of measles exposes eroding trust in health institutions and growing challenges in persuading people to protect themselves, their children and their communities.

Public health experts previously told CNBC that the return of measles ultimately boils down to fewer children getting vaccinated.

The MMR vaccine is about 97% effective against measles after two doses, while a single dose is 93% effective, according to the CDC. Yet data aggregated by the CDC shows that vaccination coverage for that shot among U.S. kindergarteners fell to 92.4% in the 2025-2026 school year, which is below the 95% threshold public health officials generally recommend to prevent sustained transmission.

The American Medical Association urged the public to get vaccinated after PAHO's announcement.

"We must treat the current measles outbreaks as an urgent call to rebuild trust in science-based recommendations and restore strong, fully-funded public health infrastructure to protect families and communities and prevent the return of diseases once eliminated," AMA President Dr. Willie Underwood said in a statement.

In a separate statement, the pharmaceutical industry's largest lobbying group called on policymakers to provide "clear, accurate information about the seriousness of measles infection and the effectiveness of vaccines against it, restore and sustain high vaccination coverage in every community, strengthen vaccination efforts, and invest in disease surveillance and rapid outbreak response."

"We have the tools to eliminate measles again and what we need now is the commitment to use them," said Dr. Michael Ybarra, chief medical officer of the Pharmaceutical Research and Manufacturers of America.

Measles isn't just a public health threat — it's also a huge financial burden on the healthcare system, CNBC reported.

Based on this year's case count, a new tool developed by researchers at Johns Hopkins University estimates an economic cost of nearly $83 million for the outbreaks. That total includes $56 million in public health response costs, almost $25 million in lost productivity and more than $1.8 million in direct medical expenses.

The U.S. now joins Canada, Spain and the U.K., which all recently lost their measles-free status after a review by experts at the World Health Organization. While the U.S. dropped out of the WHO, it remains a member of PAHO, its Americas branch.

#150. Is Apple the safe haven investors think it is? Here's a way to trade it with less risk

For months, Apple has been the undisputed haven of the technology sector. As the industry grappled with the brutal reality of Nvidia's post-Huang future, investors blindly rotated into Apple, comforting themselves with the narrative that the Cupertino giant was uniquely immune to speculative overspending on artificial intelligence.

While peers bled capital into server farms and data centers. At almost 37x forward earnings estimates, the stock is now priced for perfection, fully valued at precisely the moment the rest of the market is beginning to see multiples contract aggressively. Investors tend to huddle in the shelter of perceived safe havens, but that perceived safety can crack quickly in an adverse news cycle, as Nvidia shareholders recently learned.

One part of the news cycle finally caught up to Apple.

We've recently witnessed a bizarre parade of catastrophic system failures: the massive Microsoft outage, the FAA grounding flights, and cascading disruptions across major banking networks. Through it all, Apple was immune. Until today, when a widespread outage struck, knocking out iCloud, Apple Pay, and core support systems. The official explanation? Yet another internal update gone wrong.

It's undeniably odd how all these major tech infrastructure failures are being chalked up to routine upgrades, all clustering around the exact same narrow timeframe. Without venturing too far into the conspiratorial, the sheer coincidence of these cascading digital blackouts suggests more systemic fragility than we may have realized. That's one thing at less than 10x forward earnings, as Nvidia is, or even at a market multiple. But Apple is twice as expensive as the S&P.

Technically, Apple is balancing on a knife's edge. Shares have been relentlessly hugging the upper end of several channel-based technical indicators, such as the Bollinger Band. Apple has been priced at a steep premium for being immune to what ails other struggling stocks, but is now demonstrating similar vulnerabilities. Investors should ask themselves: what's the upside? That Apple trades at more than 40x forward earnings? 45x? Even at that rich multiple, that would represent only 18-19% upside from here. If the market suddenly decided that Apple's premium to the market would be just 50%, instead of 100%, that would be less than 28x forward earnings, a nearly 27% haircut to the current share price.

Despite the high multiple, options prices are low versus the market. Market implied volatility is well above average (around the 93rd percentile). Apple implied volatility is actually just below the 1-year average. The price of index options is in the 98th percentile relative to Apple options.

Because the stock is nominally expensive (translation? The share price is high in dollar terms), buying outright naked puts is highly capital-intensive. This is exactly where a put spread makes sense. By buying an at-the-money or slightly out-of-the-money put and simultaneously selling a lower-strike put, traders can dramatically reduce the upfront capital cost. More importantly, this structure blunts the negative impact of theta, or time decay, ensuring you don't bleed premium while waiting for the technical breakdown to materialize. Furthermore, taking advantage of the natural put skew—where further out-of-the-money puts trade at relatively higher implied volatility—lets you finance your bearish bet efficiently by selling that inflated premium.

Apple's historic premium is built on a foundation of immunity to the chaos infecting the rest of the market. Today's outage proved the company may be more vulnerable than investors appreciate.

#148. Security flaw found in Java, world's third-largest programming language

A tech start-up used AI to uncover a critical software bug in Java that had existed for more than a decade (generic photo)

A cyber-security firm has used artificial intelligence (AI) to uncover a critical flaw in the hugely popular computer programming language Java.

Tech start-up Calif - which recently identified a cyber-security flaw in another language, PHP - found the bug in Java's memory functions.

Java is a popular programming language used by nearly all of the world's top companies such as Apple and Amazon, as well as many banks, hospitals and governments.

It is meant to make the IT world more secure by managing your memory online, said computer scientist Professor Toby Walsh, which is why "this is such an unlikely and serious flaw".

The discovery comes after a string of serious technology issues, including a massive Microsoft outage.

On Tuesday, the US National Security Agency said its internal communications went offline on Tuesday due to a "scheduled routine maintenance".

The NSA did not specify whether its outage was linked to the identified Java bug.

Apple also reported multiple services suffered full outages due to an internal update, while a network failure grounded all flights in the US.

US Secretary of State Marco Rubio said there was "zero evidence" the incidents were related.

With the help of AI, Calif discovered a serious security vulnerability it has called Java4Shell, which allowed an adversary to remotely execute unwanted commands to Java's memory system.

In this case, the adversary was OpenAI's recently-released model GPT-6 Cyber, which is not yet available to the public.

Oracle, the owner of Java, released a "critical patch update" on Tuesday, which it said contained security vulnerability fixes - including for this flaw.

At this stage it is unclear if any platforms or companies have been affected by the latest incident.

But the Java flaw remains a huge concern for cyber-security experts, who say overloading a system's memory is one of the most common ways for hackers to exploit or crash a system.

The BBC has contacted Oracle for comment.

What is PHP and why did it spark a global cyber-security scramble?

US National Security Agency's classified internal network reportedly goes offline

Nearly every critical sector, including banking, national security systems, aviation and telecommunications, relies on Java.

First released in 1995, the programming language and computing system is now the "beating heart of the modern economy", Calif said in its investigation.

One of its main functions is to manage and protect memory for you, Walsh, a leading researcher in AI, told the BBC.

Any time you transfer information on the internet, receive a file from a user or need to log into an IT system with a password, the platform you are using is likely relying on a Java program.

The serious flaw could comprise "critical financial systems, customers and organisation's data," cyber-security expert Dr Pedram Hayati said.

In Australia alone, almost every bank uses Java for managing critical transaction data, Hayati added.

The latest incidents have exposed how nearly every critical sector - including national security systems, aviation and telecommunications - relies on legacy infrastructure online.

Earlier this week, thousands of customers at major US banks, including JP Morgan Chase, Wells Fargo and Bank of America, reported trouble accessing services.

The Java discovery follows a critical cyber-security flaw identified in the PHP programming language, also identified by Calif.

PHP is the code that websites like Wikipedia use to collect information and present it in a way you can read on the web.

Surrey Centre for Cyber Security Prof Alan Woodward said the PHP flaw affected some 30 million websites, leaving them vulnerable to malicious attacks.

At the time, website owners were urged to check if their sites were affected and, if so, they were told to install updates to patch the issue or consider going offline.

The PHP flaws went undetected for at least a decade, which Walsh said is similar to what has happened with Java.

"It's been hiding in plain sight for 12 years", he said.

"AI is now exposing more flaws much more quickly," Walsh said including ones that may have been so obscure and difficult that humans could not have discovered them alone.

Earlier in November, OpenAI president Greg Brockman announced his company was pivoting 25% of its staff to focus on cyber-security demands.

Are we too reliant on big tech companies?

#145. FAA pauses installation of new routers after ground stop

The Federal Aviation Administration said Tuesday it would pause the installation of new routers after problems with the technology led the agency to issue a rare nationwide ground stop on Friday.

Tens of thousands of flights were affected by the ground stop, which halted departing flights for more than four hours. It was the longest halt to U.S. flight departures since 9/11.

Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said Sunday that an overloaded router installed in southern Arkansas as part of an upgrade to a system overseen by L3Harris Technologies appeared to overwhelm systems that feed radar and other data for air traffic control.

The Wall Street Journal first reported the news about the pause.

The FAA and Department of Transportation have said L3Harris was working to convert the communications network from copper wires to fiber optic.

Democratic members of the Senate Committee on Commerce, Science and Transportation this week asked the FAA and the Department of Transportation for clarity on what went wrong.

"Identifying the device that failed does not establish why it failed," the senators wrote in a statement. "Modernization cannot come at the expense of operational safety."

The agencies have said they are working to figure out the root cause of the issue and have reiterated that there is no indication of a cyberattack or outside threat.

The senators also asked for a briefing with the FAA, L3Harris and Juniper Networks, which is making the new devices, by Monday. They asked the companies to lay out why the operational systems lacked sufficient redundancy and answer other questions about what led to the outage.

CNBC has reached out to L3Harris and Juniper Networks for comment.

Duffy on Friday called for Congress to approve $50 billion in funding for upgrades to the aging U.S. air traffic control system, more than he has previously asked for. Democratic lawmakers pushed back on the request, citing unspent money from existing funding, which includes Congress' approval last year of $12.5 billion for airport and air traffic control improvements over five years.

Senate Commerce Chair Ted Cruz, R-Texas, said Tuesday that he thinks there will be support for air traffic control modernization, but that the allocation should be specifically for safety upgrades.

"I don't think the full $50 billion will be funded," he said. "Some of that is not safety related, but we just discussed there, and what I expressed is that the request needs to be focused on safety modernization."

Duffy dismissed the idea that the router issue had to do with the introduction of artificial intelligence tools and said it was "completely unfounded" because the AI system is not in communication with the defective router. He added that the FBI is helping to investigate the root cause of the issue.

Aging equipment and a shortage of air traffic controllers have for years angered airline executives, who have complained about the cost of resulting disruptions.

— CNBC's Phil LeBeau, Leslie Josephs and Justin Papp contributed to this report.
