#144. Fed’s Waller says central bank may need to ‘shock people’ with half-point rate hike

Federal Reserve Governor Christopher Waller on Wednesday called for aggressive action against inflation, saying the central bank needs to "shock people" as prices continue to rise more than he expected.

In the near term, the policymaker told CNBC in a live interview that a half percentage point increase in the Fed's benchmark rate is probably necessary at the Dec. 8-9 meeting of the Federal Open Market Committee.

He said the central bank needs to reverse the three quarter-point reductions from last year and may need to do more.

"I'm very, very concerned about inflation. It's kind of headed in the wrong direction, and it's spreading out," Waller told CNBC's Steve Liesman during a "Squawk Box" interview. "If you really want to get it down, you've got to start taking more aggressive actions."

"You might need to, like, shock people," he said.

Futures prices are fully pricing in a quarter percentage point hike at the FOMC's December meeting. They also are anticipating another move in January, followed by at least one additional increase before the end of 2027, according to the CME Group's FedWatch gauge.

Waller said the Fed wouldn't necessarily have to approve a half-point hike in December if it raised rates by a quarter point and signaled another move coming in January. However, Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh has been against that kind of forward guidance on grounds that it ties policymakers' hands.

"If you're not going to signal this very clearly to markets, you're going in January, my view is then you have to go 50 [basis points] in December to put it in place and get it done," he said.

Recent statements from other central bankers indicate a range of opinions, though most agree that more policy tightening is needed to bring down inflation.

In a speech Tuesday, St. Louis Fed President Alberto Musalem suggested as many as four hikes by the spring of 2027. Some Fed officials, including regional Presidents Beth Hammack of Cleveland and Lorie Logan of Dallas, have endorsed more gradual tightening.

However, continued inflation above the Fed's 2% target might push officials into a more aggressive stance.

The FOMC approved its first interest rate hike in more than three years in September, and then held rates steady at its October meeting. While inflation has continued to run hot since then, other Fed officials seem less eager to drop the policy hammer as heavily as Waller.

"I don't think the situation calls for dramatic action," New York Fed President John Williams said Tuesday in a conversation with Axios. "I think what we've been doing over the last past few months is ... working well. We're collecting the data, analyzing it, having the debate and making a decision."

The Fed in 2025 approved three rate cuts amid worries over the labor market and the belief that inflation would continue to moderate toward the central bank's 2% goal.

However, inflation reignited earlier this year after the strikes the U.S. and Israel launched against Iran in late February. Energy prices spiked while tariff impacts lingered, keeping most inflation readings above 3%.

"First of all, you've got to take back the three insurance cuts we put in last year," Waller said. "If the policy rate last summer in 2025 wasn't bringing inflation down, getting back to that isn't going to bring it down. So we may have to go above ... three hikes plus maybe some more to get it to start actually coming back down."

The FOMC later Wednesday will release minutes from the October meeting.

#141. Speed camera fault may have allowed drivers to escape fines

Further problems with the accuracy of speed cameras on English roads have been uncovered which may have allowed drivers to slip through the cracks and escape fines.

National Highways discovered issues with speed cameras last year, affecting 154 out of 400 cameras but, following an investigation, said it had found another fault on units monitoring roads including those running between Huntingdon and Cambridge.

The Home Office has revoked approval that allows footage from the cameras to be used as evidence in legal proceedings. US firm Verra Mobility operates the cameras but National Highways said it will now end the contract.

Roads minister Justin Madders said: "We can't take chances with this.

"If we're going to prosecute people, we need to be confident the system is working as it should. That's why we've taken the action we have."

In a statement, Verra Mobility said it "remains fully committed to delivering reliable, innovative technology that helps make roads safer".

The fault affected all of the variable speed cameras on smart motorways, and a section of the A14 between Huntingdon and Cambridge as well as the A1 approach junction to the A14.

A spokesperson for National Highways said that Verra's camera system had been selected on the basis that it maintained Home Office approval.

The agency said that since that was no longer the case "we are ending our contractual relationship to use the cameras for enforcement".

It added: "We are considering all further commercial and legal options that may be appropriate."

The BBC has contacted Verra Mobility for comment.

Madders said that the enforcement of speed limits continues to be conducted through a range of different tactics, including mobile cameras as well as marked and unmarked police patrols.

But he admitted that the camera system will not be fully fixed until sometime next year.

Rod Dennis, senior policy officer at the RAC motoring organisation, said: "Malfunctioning speed cameras on smart motorways do more than just let speeding drivers get away with breaking the law and endangering lives â they further undermine confidence in the very types of road we know drivers already have serious misgivings about."

There are 4,000 cameras across the entire road network.

In December 2025, National Highways, which runs England's motorways, blamed an "anomaly" in how variable speed cameras were interacting with speed limit signs on some A roads and motorways.

For example, on a road where the speed limit increases, a driver may see a sign showing 60mph but the camera recording it may still be working on the basis of a previous 40mph speed limit.

The fault led to 2,650 wrongful speed camera activations between 2021 and the end of last year.

Those who had been wrongfully fined have been reimbursed.

As National Highways continued to test the system, it discovered a further problem "relating to how some of these cameras may activate in specific and rare circumstances".

It said that according to its calculations, "the circumstances in which the latest issue could occur are extremely rare, and there is no evidence to suggest it has impacted enforcement".

Nevertheless, it said it was "taking immediate steps to ensure compliance is maintained at relevant locations".

It said it would put further traffic officer patrols in place to monitor the sites.

#138. Vance scraps schedule to address tech outages as Trump admin insists incidents aren't linked

Vice President JD Vance on Wednesday abruptly canceled his scheduled appearance at The New York Times DealBook Summit to instead attend meetings on a recent wave of high-profile technology outages.

The cancellation was announced shortly after a senior Trump administration official insisted that the outages, which have affected a wide range of industries and have been felt across the country and around the world, are not the result of a cyberattack or any form of coordinated action.

"We have a great deal of confidence in our assessment that these incidents are the result of upgrades of the systems, systems being patched, hardware being applied" or other "disparate" events, the official said on condition of anonymity to discuss the government's intelligence assessments.

The official briefed news outlets by phone in the wake of numerous major disruptions at large corporations and federal agencies in recent days, including a nationwide aviation ground stop, a protracted Microsoft Outlook outage and a reported blackout of the National Security Agency's classified communications network.

Other incidents include software issues at JPMorgan Chase, an outage at United Airlines that caused some flight delays and product issues at Apple.

On Tuesday, Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton told CNBC that the outages are not a cyberattack and "not a coordinated effort by a nation-state."

He gave a similar assurance on Wednesday to the House Homeland Security Committee.

"We have no indication that these incidents are related, that they're coordinated," Clayton said. "We also have no indication that they're result of a state-sponsored malign actor."

But the sheer volume of incidents in the past week alone has raised alarm bells among consumers and tech experts alike, with some wondering if rapidly advancing artificial intelligence models are to blame.

The senior Trump administration official, asked on Wednesday's call to what degree AI was involved in the outages, said it is "challenging to answer that question across all of these incidents that have been reported."

Sen. Mark Warner, D-Va., vice chairman of the Senate Intelligence Committee, told CNBC on Wednesday morning he wants "better coordination" on AI safety.

"I understand why people are freaked out," Warner said. "The one way we could lose this AI race and [see] all of these companies' valuations plummet dramatically is if we have an AI tool run amok, or an AI-sponsored cyberattack that creates harm."

His comments came hours before before Anthropic is scheduled to price its initial public offering for the first major AI industry IPO.

Warner advocated for a "pre-testing regime" for new AI models that establishes guardrails and helps ensure a "culture of safety built in" to the industry."

He said the U.S. is "lucky" that it has so far avoided a major AI-generated cyberattack or a crisis with a rogue agent that goes beyond incidents like the Hugging Face breach earlier this year.

— CNBC's Samantha Subin contributed to this report.

#137. Government backs welfare shake-up to prevent 'lost generation'

The government has backed plans by former minister Alan Milburn for sweeping reforms to prevent "losing a generation" of young people to a life on benefits.

Milburn proposes spending billions on tailored support for under 25s not in employment, education or training (Neets), to be part-funded by cuts to some benefits.

He claims his plan, which also proposes tax breaks for firms and major education reforms, will get 440,000 young people into jobs by 2036.

Prime Minister Andy Burnham said he endorsed the "principles" of the Milburn review and was confident he could persuade Labour MPs to back legislation to implement its measures.

The PM told Sky News he would set out a full response to the review next month, after he had received a separate report into Personal Independence Payments (Pips) by Social Security Minister Sir Stephen Timms.

But he added: "I have never in my years in politics seen a wholesale, whole government approach to supporting the next generation.

"That is what this represents, and I am absolutely confident that I can take this vision and get people behind it, indeed get the country behind it, because we will not place Britain in the position I want it to be in if we have a generation of young people growing up without hope for the future."

The government has made Milburn the head of a new new youth opportunities unit to deliver on his recommendations.

Conservative leader Kemi Badenoch said her party agreed with Milburn's "diagnosis" but claimed Labour had "made the problem much worse for young people" by increasing employers' National Insurance contributions and the youth minimum wage.

There are few signs so far that Burnham will face a backlash from his own MPs of the scale that blocked his predecessor Sir Keir Starmer's planned Â£5bn welfare cuts last year.

But some left-wing Labour MPs and the Unison trade union have warned proposals to cut health-related benefits will drive some young people into poverty.

Work and Pensions Secretary Pat McFadden told MPs that "in future" the welfare state should be judged "not just by a snapshot of people's monthly incomes" but "but by the story of people's lives, and by their ability to achieve their full potential".

McFadden, who was flanked by Burnham and Chancellor John Healey during his statement in the Commons, said the government rejected claims that young people were "snowflakes and shirkers".

The million who were not in work or training had been robbed of the "prospect of a good future" and that was something that demanded "much more" than a conventional government response, he told MPs.

He said the government would publish draft proposals on welfare reform early next year, with legislation to follow.

In his wide-ranging 250 page report, external, Milburn proposes:

An "entirely new benefits system" for under-25s, which would end the health-top up of Universal Credit and Personal Independence Payments (Pip), the main disability benefit in England and Wales

Instead, a new "youth allowance" would be set at the basic rate of Universal Credit for under-25s, with a higher rate for those living independently

A reduction in employers' National Insurance costs for 21- to 24-year-olds, starting in the retail and hospitality sectors, meaning employing those young people would be cheaper

A huge expansion in post-18 college places and a legal right to work, education or training

A new, locally-led Youth Opportunity Service in England to give young people one named advisor and a personalised participation plan

Primary and secondary schools required to identify and support pupils at risk of becoming Neet, and schools obliged to offer work experience

The report calls for the government to spend around Â£5.8bn in total by 2036-37 on extra mental health and employment support.

And it urges ministers to "kickstart the reform programme" next year, with an initial Â£500m, from "repriotising" existing spending.

At a glance: Key proposals from Milburn's Neets report

Why Milburn's Neets reforms will only save Â£200m a year by 2036

Where are the most jobs for 18-24 year-olds?

Milburn has said his plans are not about saving money - and his report says the government will not see overall savings from reduced benefit bills and higher tax receipts until 2036/27, when it will claw back Â£200m.

But by then, it assumes the share of 16â24-year-olds that are Neet will have gone down from 13% - one of the highest in Europe - to 5%, the lowest in the UK since records began.

Launching his report earlier on Tuesday, Milburn said "half measures will not do" because "the scale of the problem is just too big for that".

He added: "Britain is at risk of losing a generation of young people, not because they lack talent, not because they lack ambition, not because they do not want to work.

"This is not a crisis of aspiration; it is a crisis of opportunity."

Milburn's report has no specific blueprint for Scotland, Wales or Northern Ireland, where education, skills, health and local government are devolved.

He said the UK and devolved governments "should agree common definitions, a shared set of headline outcomes and a regular joint review of progress".

Scotland's Education Secretary MÃ iri McAllan said: "Where there is opportunity to collaborate with the UK government and others for the benefit of young people in Scotland, we will take it."

But she argued many of the reforms recommended for England, such as stronger vocational training, were already in place in Scotland.

A spokesperson for the Welsh government said: "We stand ready to work with the UK government to ensure Wales benefits fully from any UK-wide measures that improve life chances through access to rewarding work."

Reform UK leader Nigel Farage said he agreed "with the general drift of the Milburn report, but it's too little and too slow".

"We have a fundamental problem in Britain," he added. "Too many people are being encouraged to go on benefits, and training and apprenticeship schemes that take far too long."

Lib Dem treasury spokesperson Daisy Cooper said if the government wanted to get people into work, it should back high street firms "that give many their first job", scrap the "unfair jobs tax" and negotiate a new growth and defence partnership with the EU.

Green Party leader Zack Polanski described Milburn's proposals as a "flimsy sticking plaster" that intend to "slash support for disabled young people while subsidising corporations to hire cheap labour".

#134. Jeff Bezos says he was 'heavily involved' in casting Robert Pattinson as new Bond

Watch: Jeff Bezos on involvement in casting Robert Pattinson as James Bond

Jeff Bezos has said he was "heavily involved" in casting Robert Pattinson as the new James Bond.

Speaking at an event in California, the Amazon chairman said he was originally "biased against" Pattinson - who starred in many blockbuster films like Batman and the Twilight series - "because he's already so famous".

The role of 007 has typically gone to an actor who is not as well known at the time of casting. But Bezos said in screen tests, "it was so obvious he is James Bond".

This will be the first 007 film in the official series not under the creative control of the Broccoli family, who had overseen the franchise since the first movie in 1962. Amazon purchased MGM, which co-owns the franchise, in 2022.

Robert Pattinson: How the Twilight star became 007

'Good Bond vibes' - Hollywood locals react to Robert Pattinson cast as new 007

"There was something special about Rob", Bezos said at Semafor Silicon Valley & The World Event on Tuesday.

Pattinson was unveiled as the franchise's seventh James Bond on Monday, taking over from actor Daniel Craig. He will star in the 26th Bond movie, due to be released in 2028.

In a statement, the London-born actor said: "James Bond is one of the most legendary characters in cinema, and to be entrusted with 007 is the honour of a lifetime."

The next Bond film will be directed by Denis Villeneuve, but its title and exact release date have not yet been announced.

The announcement comes after years of intense speculation about who will take over from Craig in one of the most high-profile - and highly scrutinised - roles in entertainment.

Robert Pattinson was unveiled as the franchise's seventh James Bond on Monday

Several other actors, including Josh O'Connor, Callum Turner and Jack Lowden, were reported to be in the frame for the role as screen tests took place in recent weeks.

But Amazon MGM ultimately went with a household name, choosing a more bankable movie star who is widely seen as a safe pair of hands.

"We ran a really tight process and made the final decision on Friday," Bezos said.

There were five finalists who went through "real, old-fashioned screen tests", acting out three different scenes in full costume, with real sets and other actors, he said.

"By the time we got to this list of five, they were all amazing. But there was something special about Rob and his performance," Bezos added. "It was immediately un-debated. Denis [Villeneuve], the director â¦ we all gravitated toward Rob."

The pick comes amid widespread Hollywood cuts and consolidation, with studios struggling to get people into cinemas.

Hollywood insiders have said Pattinson was viewed as a safe pick to bring audiences in, rather than taking a risk on a new actor who could grow with the franchise.

"Maybe he's boring. Maybe Hollywood would be passing up a chance to anoint a badly needed new star. But with so much at stake for everyone involved, that all makes him the obvious pick," Hollywood journalist Matthew Belloni, who predicted the pick, wrote in his newsletter.

The BBC's Regan Morris contributed to this report.

#133. Cantor Fitzgerald's Nvidia business interests targeted in Democrats' Lutnick probe

Democrats are scrutinizing the business of Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick's adult sons as they investigate why the top Trump administration official allegedly resisted cracking down on companies suspected of diverting Nvidia's advanced AI chips to China.

The probe unveiled Wednesday ratchets up the pressure Lutnick was already facing from inside and outside the Trump administration. It provides the latest sign that Nvidia, the scandal-scarred AI behemoth, will be embroiled in congressional Democrats' coming anti-corruption crusade, as well as a wider bipartisan effort to clamp down on AI chip sales to China.

Reps. Jamie Raskin, D-Md., and Gregory Meeks, D-N.Y., the respective ranking members of the House Judiciary and Foreign Affairs Committees, are demanding reams of records from Cantor Fitzgerald, the investment firm Brandon and Kyle Lutnick took over from their father after he joined President Donald Trump's Cabinet in February 2025.

"We seek to understand whether any actions or decisions by Secretary Lutnick concerning export controls, enforcement measures, or national security policies may have been improperly influenced by Cantor Fitzgerald's holdings or business interests," the Democrats wrote in a letter sent Tuesday to Brandon Lutnick.

At the time of Howard Lutnick's confirmation, Cantor had amassed a stake in Nvidia reportedly valued around $1.2 billion, one of its largest holdings for its clients. Between September 2025 and March 2026, the firm reported a nearly 50% decline in its Nvidia holdings, a CNBC analysis of securities filings found.

In the same time period, Lutnick had reportedly been pressed to take action against Megaspeed, a Singapore-based tech firm accused of transferring Nvidia AI chips to China. The New York Times reported in October 2025 that the Commerce Department was investigating Megaspeed's ties to Beijing.

Lutnick "repeatedly stood in the way" of implementing controls on Megaspeed and other companies suspected of funneling cutting-edge chips to China, Axios reported Nov. 11.

"Secretary Lutnick's reported obstruction may have resulted in billions of dollars of restricted AI chips being diverted to China," read the Democrats' letter, which was also signed by Rep. Sydney Kamlager-Dove, D-Calif., the ranking member of the Foreign Affairs Subcommittee on South and Central Asia.

Lutnick's reported dithering may also have propped up Nvidia share prices for months, "preventing devastating losses for your company," the Democrats wrote.

"If these media reports are accurate, the American people deserve to know: Did the Secretary of Commerce seriously and perhaps irreversibly compromise U.S. national security interests to make money for his adult children and Cantor Fitzgerald?" they asked.

There is no known evidence that Lutnick's sons had advance or insider knowledge or information about Nvidia or the investigations surrounding it.

Cantor, in a statement to CNBC, called the House Democrats' letter "inaccurate" and said any allegations that its work has influenced government decisions are "baseless."

"Cantor holds no material proprietary position in Nvidia. Cantor's Nvidia holdings arise primarily from equity swaps executed for clients. We buy the underlying shares to hedge those trades. The decline in our holdings since early 2025 reflects the unwinding of those hedges as clients closed or reduced their swap positions, not a Cantor decision to exit a proprietary position," the company said in the statement.

"As a global investment bank, Cantor serves clients across many sectors," read the statement, which called any work with Southeast Asia-based companies "ordinary-course investment banking business."

"We are confident that a full review of the facts will demonstrate that we have operated our business ethically and in full compliance with all applicable rules and regulations," Cantor's statement said.

The new probe is among the first to be announced since the blue-wave midterm election, which will deliver Democrats control of the House and Senate come January.

Nvidia's stock has dropped significantly in recent weeks, straining the tech sector it both leads and helps finance, while raising broader concerns about the stability of an economy that has been fueled by the AI boom.

Jensen Huang admitted to an affair without specifying it was with former Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, whom U.S. officials have reportedly identified as a Chinese spy. Alice Huang and Jensen Huang are not related. Jensen Huang has reportedly engaged in at least two other affairs with Nvidia subordinates. On Tuesday, Jensen Huang wrote on X that he would return as CEO if the company's board asked him back. In its earnings call Tuesday evening, Nvidia Chair Harvey Jones said the board does not intend to reinstate Huang.

In addition to the records, Raskin, Meeks and Kamlager-Dove are demanding Cantor Fitzgerald immediately take "all necessary measures to prevent the destruction, alteration, deletion, or modification of any documents or communications related to this matter." They asked for confirmation by Nov. 30 that appropriate preservation measures have been taken.

Their action illustrates how aggressively Democrats plan to investigate all facets of the Trump administration as they home in on alleged corruption by Trump, his allies and his family.

It's not just Democrats asking questions — Republicans have also demanded answers from Huang, Lutnick and other Nvidia and Commerce officials as the Megaspeed scandal has unfurled. Huang is slated to testify before multiple GOP-led House panels on Dec. 1, while Sen. Tim Scott, R-S.C., has also summoned both Huang and Howard Lutnick to testify.

Earlier Tuesday, House Foreign Affairs Committee Chairman Brian Mast, R-Fla., sent his own letter demanding information from Under Secretary of Commerce Jeffrey Kessler regarding possible chip smuggling by Megaspeed and its affiliate Speedmatrix.

"More and more reports are coming to light of possibly extraordinary levels of chip smuggling conducted by Megaspeed and its broader corporate network, all under Jensen Huang's nose," Mast said in a statement.

#131. How important are Nvidia and Jensen Huang to the US economy?

Nvidia and its co-founder Jensen Huang have been at the centre of the artificial intelligence (AI) spending boom.

No company or chief executive has arguably been more consequential to the development of AI in recent years, with the US microchip manufacturer under Huang positioning itself as a critical cog in the development of the world-changing technology.

That partnership has now ended following Huang's resignation over revelations of extramarital affairs, one of which is alleged to have been with a Chinese spy.

The company has been rocked by the scandal, with hundreds of billions of dollars wiped off its stock market value and questions swirling about the extent of the impact of Huang's departure.

While Nvidia is not as much of a household name as other Big Tech players such as Apple, Amazon and Microsoft, the company has been just as integral to the booming US stock market of recent years.

The billions of dollars being funnelled into AI and the construction of data centres has bolstered the American economy, with Nvidia being one of the biggest spenders.

Originally focusing on chips which process graphics for computer games, it pivoted to making the chips used in AI systems and now dominates the market.

It is now one the most valuable firms, worth $3.8 trillion (Â£2.9 trillion), and on Tuesday said its rapid growth is poised to continue as the firm's quarterly sales doubled from a year earlier.

But there's now an air of uncertainty over Nvidia's future direction. Huang played a major role in the company, leading its shift towards AI, and maintained a firm grip on the company, with up to 200 people directly reporting to him.

Dan Coatsworth, head of markets at investment firm AJ Bell, says because Huang was also a key figure in "flying the flag" to drum up enthusiasm and investment for the wider AI industry, his departure posed a "real risk" that Nvidia could now decide to change its current strategy and rein in spending.

Such a move could spell a wider slowdown in AI investment, which would have an economic impact. How big? That's "unknown", Coatsworth says.

"If they decide to go with the much more cautious route... there could be a knock-on effect in that you have got various companies in the AI infrastructure space suddenly finding they don't have a key source of finance," he says.

Kunjan Sobhani, senior technology industry analyst at Bloomberg Intelligence, expects the impact of Huang leaving to be "limited" for now as Nvidia has strong commitments and a deep order book. But he said this picture could change in the long run.

That is because Nvidia, on its journey to the top of the AI industry, has created a spider's web of deals through what is known as "circular financing".

Not only does it sell microchips to its customers, which include the likes of Meta, SpaceX and OpenAI, it has also provided funds for them and other firms in the supply chain by either handing out loans or investing in them directly.

The practice has prompted concerns among some investors, who worry what might happen if Nvidia were to turn off the spending taps. If investment dried up, would other firms in the supply chain cut spending or jobs themselves? Would that spread to the wider US economy?

That risk is perhaps why tech bosses including Elon Musk and Tim Sweeney, the CEO of Fortnite maker Epic Games, have called for Huang's reinstatement.

Musk claimed that "critical deals" involving Nvidia's technology would be lost following Huang's resignation, which has also prompted a campaign by shareholders and supporters to "Bring Jensen Back".

Huang himself has said he is open to a return, just days after resigning.

Coatsworth says he has never seen circular financing used at this scale before, but admits there are competitive rivals around, which could insulate the wider AI industry from any potential slowdown at the firm.

When Nvidia's share price plunged in the wake of the affair revelations, the stock of its rival semiconductor makers including AMD, Broadcom and Marvell Technology jumped.

"I don't think all AI activity grinds to a halt if Nvidia slows down what it's doing and if it pulls some financing, but it does change how people would look at the whole situation," Coatsworth adds.

The risk of a slowdown has already rattled stock markets. Investors took fright at Democrat gains in the midterm elections, with many having campaigned for tougher regulation of AI firms.

Questions also remain about whether the huge sums being invested in AI will actually pay off.

Investment in AI is expected to exceed $1 trillion in 2026, according to investment bank Goldman Sachs, who said the technology would remain the "dominant driver of business investment growth" next year.

Nvidia's interim boss Colette Kress said it was "business as usual" without Huang at the helm as the company's rapid growth remains on track.

The value of the shares in Big Tech companies have cushioned the US economy from global economic shocks, including tariffs, buoying investments and pensions for people worldwide.

Despite Huang saying he would be "honoured" to return, Nvidia's board has said it is not considering bringing him back.

And while Coatsworth thinks "no CEO is irreplaceable", he admits the pressure is on for whoever succeeds him.

#130. Nvidia says it won't reinstate Huang as it reports soaring sales

Jensen Huang and Nvidia's interim CEO, Colette Kress

Nvidia has firmly ruled out letting ousted co-founder Jensen Huang return as it revealed that sales doubled to more than $100bn.

In the company's first results since Huang's dramatic exit last week, covering the three month period just before the recent scandal surfaced, interim chief executive Colette Kress said it was "business as usual". She told investors: "We've got this."

Earlier on Tuesday, Huang said he would be "honoured" to return and lead Nvidia, having quit following alleged extramarital affairs, including one with a suspected spy for China.

But Nvidia's chair Harvey Jones said: "The board does not intend to reinstate him."

Nvidia reported that sales for the three months to 25 October rose to $115.8bn (Â£86.9bn), with shares in the company jumping by 4.8% in after-hours trading.

The company's profits also doubled from a year ago rising to $65bn. Sales from data centres, a significant part of Nvidia's business, also doubled to $107bn.

The company said its rapid growth is poised to continue and revenue will rise by another 13% in the next quarter.

How important are Nvidia and Jensen Huang to the US economy?

Given the uncertainty surrounding the company after Huang's exit, financial analysts had been unsure that Nvidia would provide any forward-looking guidance.

Daniela Hathorn, a senior analyst for trading platform Capital.com, said Nvidia's results "offer a strong answer to concerns that demand for AI infrastructure is cooling" and temper immediate worries that Huang was irreplaceable as a leader.

"The harder question is whether Nvidia can preserve the strategic advantage Huang helped build," Hathorn added. "His exit is unlikely to change orders already committed, but could matter more when Nvidia makes its next round of decisions on products, partnerships and investment."

Watch: Who is suspected Chinese spy at centre of Nvidia affair investigation?

Kress seemed eager to stress that Nvidia can continue to not only succeed, but lead the ongoing AI buildout that is happening throughout the technology industry.

"It is business as usual," she said. "Our partners, our suppliers, all of them are 100% behind us."

She added that Nvidia's leadership team has "stood up very strongly over the last week... working seamlessly in all of their departments and projects".

"Our momentum is real. Our demand is accelerating. Our supply is growing. Our customers and our partners are with us, and employees are committed." Kress said before leaving the analyst call. "We've got this."

The interim chief executive also said Nvidia is no longer shipping Hopper data centre semiconductors to China.

The Hopper technology is in Nvidia's advanced H200 processors, which the firm won approval to sell in China after Huang's repeated lobbying efforts in Washington.

They had previously been restricted over concerns that selling them to China would give its industries and military an edge over the US.

Kress did not elaborate on why the shipments had been stopped.

The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

Jensen Huang says he would be 'honoured' to return as Nvidia CEO if board asked him

Jones saying earlier that the company would not answer analysts' questions about Huang or Nvidia's investigation into his conduct, he did address the search for a permanent replacement.

He said Nvidia is working with an executive search firm and is considering internal and external candidates, but that the company would not rush its selection.

"With the advanced technology and the national security dynamics in the marketplace, our new chief executive will have to navigate those waters carefully," Jones said. "The search will be thorough and we want to do it as quickly as possible, but we won't do it before we find the exact right person."

Michael Monaghan from investment firm Founder ETFs is among those who want Huang back in the top job at Nvidia.

The Nvidia investor told the BBC that he is waiting to see the results of a thorough investigation into Huang's actions, instead of relying on "salacious allegations" made in the media.

Nvidia's strong earnings are "from the machine that Jensen built" and a company tends to lose "its shine and vision" after an iconic founder leaves, Monaghan said.

#129. Heating bills, chocolate and cat food: What the inflation figures mean for you

Nobody will be celebrating the fact that prices in the UK are rising at the fastest pace for a year.

Add to that an expectation of higher household energy bills and sharpening price increases and things look grim for everyone.

But there are some signs of hope that may take the edge off the rising cost of living. Here's how.

That headline is not missing a decimal point. The latest prediction for the increase in domestic energy prices in the new year is, indeed, 26%.

Millions of homes in England, Scotland and Wales are on tariffs affected by regulator Ofgem's price cap, which is set every three months. The next change for January will be officially announced next Wednesday, 25 November.

Energy consultancy Cornwall Insight, which has a good track record with its predictions, suggests that a household using a typical amount of gas and electricity will pay Â£2,166 a year - that's an increase of Â£443 from current levels.

Not all energy use for the year is between January and March, but a big chunk is owing to the cold, dark days and nights.

How much you pay depends on your use, with heavy users of gas likely to be the hardest hit.

The government has promised a Â£100 payment for people living in homes in council tax bands A to D in England to help cover winter energy costs. Other nations are planning similar support.

The latest inflation data shows that government assistance can have an impact on the general rate of inflation, external, as recorded by the Office for National Statistics (ONS).

Domestic energy prices rose by nearly 4% in October, but this was offset in inflationary terms by a cut in VAT on electricity bills.

While the rate of inflation in general was 3.6% in the year to October, food prices only went up by 0.9%.

It is partly the result of intense competition between the supermarkets. Like many retailers, they are facing higher costs but are not passing that onto their customers in full.

There may be some scars from the recent cost-of-living spike here. Shoppers suffered a period during the big rise in inflation in 2022-23 when food prices rocketed.

Those higher prices are embedded. While food prices overall aren't coming down now, they aren't rising anywhere near as fast as back then.

The detail here is very interesting. Take chocolate: world cocoa prices are down since 2024, and now chocolate prices are falling - down 2.9%, according to the latest data.

But the ONS has also pointed to cat food as one, albeit small, driver of the faster rate of rising prices.

Should high energy and fuel costs - which are driving the rising rate of inflation - persist, there are questions over whether the cost of producing and transporting food also goes up, and the chances of that higher cost being passed on to consumers are greater.

Economists have been closely studying whether there are any signs of those so-called second-round effects - where an initial price shock spreads to wages, services and broader consumer prices. So far, there are relatively few.

If prices rise then it takes a bigger chunk of people's income and so has a deeper impact on those whose finances are already stretched, so the latest data on food prices is good news for many people.

Typical energy bill forecast to go up by Â£443 a year from January as inflation rate rises again

One set of economists who will consider whether there is evidence of second-round effects are the nine people who set interest rates, which heavily influences the cost of borrowing money - such as mortgages - as well as the returns people get on savings.

The Bank of England's Monetary Policy Committee meets again in December and, after that, in February.

Having just raised interest rates - for the first time in more than three years - to 4%, there's a widespread expectation among analysts that another hike is to come.

To be clear, we don't know if and when that will happen for sure.

The case for an increase would be if prices continue to accelerate. The Bank will want to slow that down.

The case against would be price rises remaining relatively confined to energy-related costs. Any easing of Middle East tensions could limit price rises without Bank of England intervention.

As far as wages are concerned, the latest official data suggests that rising prices are largely covered by rising pay.

That is an average - so is not true for everyone - but how long it remains the case may be key to how deep upcoming price rises cut into our personal finances.

How is the rising cost of living affecting your quality of life?

#128. Booking Holdings creates new enterprise division as AI reshapes travel planning

Booking Holdings is bringing together two of its business-to-business travel operations in a new division it hopes will be transformational, betting that a larger, coordinated partner platform can generate growth as artificial intelligence changes how travelers discover and book trips.

The new enterprise unit, dubbed Booking Partner Division, will combine Booking.com Strategic Partnerships with Rocket Travel by Agoda, and is set to begin operating in January. Agoda CEO Omri Morgenshtern will lead the unit, which aims to help businesses such as banks, airlines and online travel companies sell trips or build branded experiences for their customers.

"We realize that if we focus our efforts both on the product side, but also on the go-to-market side, then we can potentially grow faster and serve better partners," Morgenshtern told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

He said existing partners will find new and better products, which will also help grow the partner base.

The move gives Booking a single organization to coordinate technology, travel supply and commercial expertise across business relationships. The company says partners will be able to use its global supply and technology through white-label platforms, integrated applications, branded solutions and hotel-chain rate distribution.

The portfolio includes more than 4.7 million properties, 33 million accommodation listings, more than 500 airlines and more than 200 car rental providers, according to the company.

Competitor Expedia Group has seen notable growth by prioritizing its enterprise efforts, including a strategy launched in 2022 to make its technology platform available to business partners looking to add travel to their own offerings.

From 2018 to 2023, Expedia’s business-to-business revenue grew at a 16% compound annual rate, while adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization, or EBITDA, for the division grew at a 28% rate, the company said in 2024.

In 2025, Expedia’s B2B revenue rose 18% to $4.8 billion, and its segment adjusted EBITDA increased 22% to $1.26 billion. Those figures represented about a third of Expedia’s total revenue and more than a third of its adjusted EBITDA for the full year.

Morgenshtern declined to say how much the new division is expected to contribute to Booking Holdings' overall revenue and profit, but he said the opportunity for growth is immense, estimating the total addressable market at $2 trillion.

Online travel agencies have been pressured this year as investors scrutinize how AI will change the way customers search for and then book their travel.

Companies that offer lodging, experiences and other travel and leisure are grappling with how to stay visible if AI tools become the new starting point for planning and reservations.

Disney CEO Josh D’Amaro said on the company’s earnings call Tuesday that guests already use AI to discover and plan Disney trips, and that some are using the tools to book.

“Ultimately, we want Disney to show up in those tools,” he said, adding that Disney is exploring ways to let guests make reservations directly from them.

Booking anticipates offering partners AI capabilities that draw on the company's existing travel know-how while minimizing token costs, it said.

"I see a lot of AI demos," Morgenshtern said, drawing a distinction between a product that caters to a few customers and one that's scalable and works even when overloaded by travel emergencies. He said he's "super optimistic" about the bespoke product — a vertical agent — that Booking will offer to its partners.

#127. I watched Trump, Xi and Putin gather in China. Here are my top takeaways

SHENZHEN, China — It took a trip to China for me, an American, to see U.S. President Donald Trump in person for the first time.

Trump emerged from a group of black-clad photographers and other personnel surrounding him at a welcome banquet in Shenzhen for the Asia-Pacific Economic Leaders' Meeting held Nov. 17 to 19.

Seated just a few spots to his left was Russian President Vladimir Putin who picked up a glass of red wine to join a toast led by Chinese President Xi Jinping.

"At tomorrow's Leaders' Meeting, we will discuss measures to expand mutual opening up, explore pathways to innovation-driven growth, and identify opportunities to expand practical cooperation across various fields, so as to translate more initiatives into tangible results," Xi said in Mandarin, according to a CNBC translation.

Xi was speaking broadly, but Thursday's agenda includes a bilateral meeting with Trump, and their highly anticipated trilateral meeting with Putin.

But looking at the mood in the room, it seemed that their trilateral conversation had already started Wednesday night at their long banquet table. Much of what happens at APEC, including bilateral meetings and other exchanges between leaders, takes place away from the media.

But what struck me was how little sign there was of the tension surrounding Nvidia's CEO Jensen Huang resignation, a controversy that has gripped markets for the past 10 days.

Ahead of Trump's entrance into the banquet hall, Chinese Commerce Minister Wang Wentao shared a friendly moment with U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent and other members of the American delegation.

Last week, the U.S. officials said that Trump did not plan to raise allegations of Huang's affair with an alleged Chinese spy during his meeting with Xi.

It was a striking contrast to just two months ago, when I saw photographs of Huang and Tesla CEO Elon Musk sharing a table with Trump and Xi in Washington, D.C.

And when Trump visited Beijing in May, Huang took a few minutes to speak with reporters, including me, inside the Great Hall of the People. Musk was there too, accompanied by one of his sons.

This time, neither were visible in a room that was closely watched by the world. Musk was expected to join Trump's delegation in Shenzhen, but I did not see him Wednesday evening.

Their absence from the government-heavy banquet was striking. The world's most powerful leaders may be grappling with the implications of artificial intelligence, but they certainly didn't need the industry's biggest names at the table to do so.

Trump arrives in China for historic meeting with Xi, Putin

I watched Trump, Xi and Putin gather in China. Here are my top takeaways

Why the Middle East is watching the Trump-Xi-Putin summit

China's Xi calls for removing trade and investment barriers

Putin says EU is losing industry, falling behind competition

Three things to watch as Trump, Xi and Putin meet in China

Over the months I've spent following APEC's "China Year," one thing has become clear: The forum has given Beijing an opportunity to host the U.S., Japan and other member economies at a time when unresolved tensions might otherwise have made such gatherings difficult.

But even in a year defined by talks of U.S.-China "strategic stability," the APEC events have exposed the rifts and the shifting dynamics in global power. Those dynamics were on display for the opening of the APEC CEO Summit.

Several hundred attendees, from Xiaomi CEO Lei Jun to U.S. Ambassador to China David Perdue, arrived early for the opening of the APEC CEO Summit.

After waiting in silence for at least 10 minutes, the audience welcomed Xi in a distinctly modern way: by raising their smartphones to capture the moment. The applause was rather muted.

It was much the same at the more government-heavy welcome banquet that evening. So many attendees rushed to take their own photographs of Xi and other leaders entering the room that reporters' views were largely blocked.

Following Xi's morning speech, there was a lengthy intermission before Malaysian Prime Minister Anwar Ibrahim took the stage.

"The entire multilateral framework needs to change," he said, praising China on its "rare" recognition of smaller countries.

During the tea break that followed, attendees quickly resumed mingling. Some even brought coffee and snacks back into the room as Putin prepared to speak.

They listened as the Russian president talked about his country's economic resilience and claimed Europe was losing ground. As attendees headed to lunch, one thing stood out: Neither Trump nor any other G7 leader had taken the stage at the opening event. Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney spoke later in the afternoon.

The most visible American presence during the morning session came from business leaders appearing in a promotional video ahead of Xi's speech.

And the absence of an American voice was perhaps even more noticeable at Wednesday evening's banquet.

Trump did not follow Xi with a toast of his own. This was the same famously outspoken president, who in a winding speech in Davos, had declared that the U.S. must get Greenland.

Yet on Wednesday night in China, Trump wasn't the one holding the microphone.

#125. Europe plans to catch the next computer chip wave

Photonic chips use light instead of electrons to process data

"Photonic chips are on the verge of a breakthrough," says Ton van Mol of the Dutch research institute TNO.

If van Mol is right â and TNO, the European Union and the Dutch government are backing the idea with millions of euros - then the technology could be a gamechanger.

The chips which run today's society, from your phone and TV, to the vast data centres meeting our insatiable desire for AI, are mostly electrical.

They process data by shunting electrons around tiny circuits â a single microchip produced today can contain billions of transistors, and sizes are measured in nanometres (one nanometre is a billionth of a metre).

Because they use light instead of electricity, photonic chips are faster, use less energy and generate less heat than traditional computer chips.

Van Mol hopes for an acceleration in photonics similar to the one seen in electrical chips since the 1970s.

"With optical chips, you are now where electronic chips were maybe 40 [or] 50 years ago."

American, Taiwanese and Chinese firms dominate the computer chip industry, but Europe sees the emergence of photonic chips as a fresh opportunity to lead, on what could be, a key future technology.

Which is why the European Union has committed â¬400m ($450m; Â£330m) to a project called PIX Europe, external, with the stated goal of making the continent "a global leader in emerging technology for Photonic Integrated Chips".

"Europe said 'we need to have our own manufacturing in-house'," says van Mol, who is leading a key part of the programme.

"We cannot depend like in the past that everything is manufactured in the US or China - that's not wise, so the [European] Chips Act was called into life, and one of the parts of the act is to build pilot lines to really bring the technology, which is now in labs, to the next stage - manufacturing."

Electronic and photonic chips are produced on wafers made up of hundreds of individual chips. Factories with machines that can handle bigger wafers can churn out chips more efficiently.

"[Photonic chip] manufacturing worldwide is [currently] done on wafers which are four inches (10cm) in size, and the next step is to scale-up production to a six-inch wafer," says van Mol.

His team are already building a 10,000 sq m factory to produce six-inch indium phosphide wafers on the High Tech Campus in the Dutch city of Eindhoven.

It's due to fully open in 2030 and will be a major step forward, not just for photonics in Europe, but around the world, according to van Mol.

"That's the development TNO is going to do, in a pilot line which is really serious.

"In 2030, we hope that we have a stable, reproducible, high-yield process, and then the intention is that the pilot line [opens] to industrial parties who can make commercial production out of it."

Netherland's based ASML is a crucial supplier of chip-making equipment

Europe does have one crucial player in the current computer chip industry - ASML.

The Netherlands-based tech giant is the only company in the world able to build the machines needed to make the most sophisticated semiconductors.

Which is why ASML products will sit at the heart of the pilot line in Eindhoven - it's no coincidence that van Mol's project is based around the corner from ASML's headquarters.

"The way you produce these [photonic] chips is very similar to the way you produce electrical or electronic chip," says van Mol.

"You use the same type of equipment, you also need a high-end clean room, you also use wafers, so the production method is very similar to the traditional semiconductor technology."

ASML itself is keen to explore the opportunities that the emerging technology of photonics could offer.

"Europe has a strong position in photonics research. The next challenge is translating those innovations into industrial-scale manufacturing," says Stanislas Baron, who is the head of ASML's EU/US Customer Team.

Peter Wennik says TNO's pilot production line is a "launchpad" for European tech

ASML's unique success has helped build an influential and lucrative network of companies and institutions devoted to semiconductor technology in and around Eindhoven, and it's a model for what Europe is trying to achieve with photonic chips.

The Eindhoven project has the backing of former ASML chief executive Peter Wennink, author of an influential report for the Dutch government called The Roadmap to Future Prosperity, external, which noted that the market for photonics was expected to grow 10 to 20 times in the next 10 years.

In a news release backing the new pilot line, Wennink described it as "a launchpad for Europe's future digital economy".

"By investing in scalable production capacity, we are creating an ecosystem that can help shape the global technological direction," he said.

Of course, Europe is not alone in looking to the potential of photonics to earn a global economic advantage.

In June this year, US photonics company Coherent announced what it called "the world's first and largest volume-production six-inch indium phosphide manufacturing platform" at its plant in Sherman, in Texas.

The expansion is backed by chip manufacturing giant Nvidia, external, as well as $50 million public funds under the US Chips Act.

It's an illustration of how quickly the market is moving, and how central photonic chips could be to the continuing race to retain an influential stake in AI and technological development more generally.

Europe's bid to establish itself as a global leader in photonic chips â exemplified by the Eindhoven project â is the kind of strategy that makes sense in a globalised economy, says Joris Teer, policy analyst at the European Institute for Security Studies in Brussels.

If the continent can take a lead in photonic chips, then future developments in AI and data will partly be dependent on Europe.

"Europe knows that its high energy and labour costs, skilled workforce shortages, and lack of private and public capital make an across-the-board reindustrialisation strategy impossible", Teer says.

"To retain influence, ensuring dependence on European parties rather than European autonomy is key."

Once up and running, the Eindhoven project will provide a pilot production line which can be used multiple companies.

It will be open to businesses across Europe and beyond, who can design and build chips without having to develop their own expensive production facilities.

"What we want to establish here is what we call a foundry model; there's no intention to have our own product," Ton van Mol says.

Instead, they will produce to order for outside customers.

It's the same idea which, at huge scale, has brought Taiwanese-based giant TSMC a position of global dominance in the manufacture of microchips.

Photonic chips are already in use, in highly specialised areas in data centres and in Lidar (light detection and ranging) sensor technology, for instance.

For van Mol of TNO, helping the industry to scale up, by easing production challenges and increasing capacity, is the key step to moving them into the tech we use in our daily lives, like phones and laptops, and to drive innovation in areas like medical scanners and 6G communications.

"The demand is there", says van Mol, "and the end goal is to have, in Europe, manufacturing of these chips, which is strategically very important because these chips [could] become the backbone of all data and telecom."

Graduates are managing AI - but are they learning much else?

#123. Retail investor enthusiasm for Anthropic IPO to rival SpaceX debut

Retail investor Dan Shetty is so eager to get in on the Anthropic initial public offering that he "drained" his Roth IRA and moved roughly $12,000 from Fidelity to Robinhood for a chance to participate.

"I don't want to tell my grandkids that I didn't invest in Anthropic and I didn't invest in OpenAI during the AI bubble," he said.

Shetty's enthusiasm is shared by many, with excitement among small investors for the blockbuster IPO expected to be off the charts. Vanda Research estimates that Anthropic could see approximately $250 million of retail net buying when it starts trading Thursday, double the size of SpaceX's IPO.

"That would be far and away the largest amount of net retail buying of any stock that we've seen in our data on the first day of its listing," Jai Malhi, senior strategist at Vanda, said in an interview with CNBC.

The artificial intelligence laboratory — which will trade on the Nasdaq under ticker symbol ANT — is selling 825 million shares at an expected price range of $70 to $78 per share in the IPO, though sources told CNBC the stock is likely to price above that range. Individual investors are expected to be allocated as much as 20% in that initial offering. And if Vanda's figures prove correct, the buying enthusiasm will be big as regular trading begins.

Founded less than six years ago, Anthropic aims to raise as much as $64.4 billion, giving it a valuation of $1.405 trillion. If demand is especially strong, Anthropic could even raise more than the record $85.7 billion that SpaceX sold in its record IPO in June.

The tech trade has been in turmoil since Jensen Huang's departure last week as Nvidia CEO raised questions about the future of AI financing. Before then, a potential slowdown in data center development resulting from Democratic victories in the 2026 midterm elections, and signs of moderation in the capital expenditures boom, had already spooked investors.

These all stand in marked contrast to the market backdrop in place at the time of the SpaceX IPO. In June, both positioning and sentiment were high, Malhi said. The Anthropic IPO could see a different dynamic, however. Demand could be strong across the entire investor spectrum given that sentiment is depressed, meaning that the bar to beat expectations is "much easier to overcome," he explained.

"What we're seeing across both retail and institutional positioning is that there is plenty of dry powder to take part in this IPO," the strategist said.

For the 27-year-old New Yorker Shetty, who himself works at agentic data platform Data Workers, this is his first time vying to take part in an IPO. He said Anthropic's emphasis on "safety first" drew his interest.

"I've been telling all of my friends, 'If you have like $5, you should invest it in this stock because this is the pure-play AI leader,'" Shetty said. "I think of my father who invested in Microsoft during the dotcom bubble, and he divested from that stock, and it was such a silly thing to do."

Joe Pak, a former macro portfolio manager who is now an independent researcher outside London, is opting against participating in the Anthropic IPO for two reasons: the likelihood of limited allocation and current macroeconomic conditions.

He believes the odds are probably stacked against retail investors when it comes to the allocation of shares.

"It's very difficult as a retail investor to actually get an allocation in these blockbuster IPOs," the 36-year-old said. "You get maybe one share, and the base case is you don't get any at all. So, it's a bit of a catch-22. If you do get a lot of shares, it probably means that the institutional bid was weaker, and in that case, you don't actually want the shares at that level."

In terms of the economy, conditions "need to be favorable" to hold a position in a stock like Anthropic, he said. But with the Federal Reserve having raised interest rates in September and widely expected to do so again next month, the backdrop could be darkening.

"The risk that you have is the financial conditions deteriorating" before the company starts to grow into anticipated profit and revenue, which would justify purchase of the stock, Pak said.

While Pak is steering clear of the IPO, he did say that at a certain level, it would make sense to get into Anthropic.

Other retail investors such as Bryan Nearnberg share Pak's view.

"I'm planning to eventually buy Anthropic, just not at the IPO price, considering when the lockups expire and there's some selling pressure," said the 37-year-old Westchester County, N.Y. resident, who works in finance operations. "We are not at the end of this short-term negative headline sentiment."

IPOs are a sore spot for Nearnberg, who hasn't had much luck trading stocks on their opening day. His skepticism toward IPOs can be traced all the way back to when he was in college.

"I didn't get an allocation to the Facebook IPO, but I ended up buying it the first day of open. I got burned pretty quickly," Nearnberg told CNBC. "Same thing with Zynga. I didn't get an allocation, but [got] burned pretty quickly in terms of price."

As a result of those experiences, "I'm pretty sure we'll see a good entry price underneath the IPO for Anthropic by Memorial Day next year," Nearnberg said, citing sour sentiment toward technology and concern surrounding circular financing as short-term headwinds.

To be clear, all the retail investors CNBC spoke with are optimistic on AI as a transformative technology — even the ones sitting out the IPO. One trader said he believes in AI so much that he uses Anthropic's Claude, a conversational AI assistant, every day.

"From a price-to-sales ratio, Anthropic's like a really good deal," Faigen said. But "it's still a massive valuation for a $1.4 trillion company, and there are significantly better deals out there if you are a fundamental investor."

Faigen, who works for an agentic brokerage firm, said he's "leaning towards patience," as he believes Anthropic is going to be public "for a very long time."

"I just am not in a rush to get in on day one based off the valuation," he told CNBC.

Anthropic's valuation depends on its ability to sustain its rapid growth. In the nine months through Sept. 30, revenue reached more than $33 billion, 1,200% above the year-earlier period. Aggregate spending commitments total about $900 billion, primarily centered around computing power and infrastructure.

Breakneck growth leads plenty of retail and institutional investors to approach not just the Anthropic IPO but the entire AI trade with caution.

"A lot of it comes down to scrutiny over how much money they're spending on AI," said Bret Kenwell, U.S. investment analyst at eToro. "Investors want to see that those investments are turning into tangible returns, into tangible revenue."

— CNBC's Charlotte Morabito contributed to this report

#122. Typical energy bill forecast to go up by £443 a year from January as inflation rate rises again

Gas and electricity bills for a typical household are forecast to jump by Â£443 a year from January as the inflation rate rose to its highest level in a year.

A typical annual bill is predicted to rise by 26% in the new year, according to consultancy Cornwall Insight, ahead of the official announcement next week.

Some households will get a Â£100 payment to help with the rising cost of living, a policy which was announced in the Budget.

Prices rose 3.6% in the year to October, up from 3.5% in September mainly because of higher petrol and diesel prices, official figures show.

Inflation has been driven higher in recent months as the US-Israel war with Iran has driven up the cost of energy and fuel.

In response to prices surging, the Bank of England raised interest rates for the first time in three years earlier this month, in a bid to slow inflation to its target of 2%.

Interest rates increased from 3.75% to 4%, and the Bank signalled a further rise could be likely unless the conflict in the Middle East is resolved.

Higher interest rates lift the cost of borrowing for some mortgages, loans and credit cards, but can also lead to a better return on savings.

Emma Reynolds, chief secretary to the Treasury, said the government was providing help for households with energy costs.

VAT has been cut on electricity bills to zero until next July and the government will also hand Â£100 to 20 million homes in January.

But with annual household energy bills forecast to reach Â£2,166, Cornwall Insight's retail markets principal, Craig Lowrey, said that "ultimately this support can only chip away at the edges of a bill that continues to be pushed up by events in the Middle East".

Energy Secretary Miatta Fahnbulleh, said: "Affordability is my number one priority, and I will continue driving towards cheap, clean power."

Sophie, who manages a cafe in Brighton, told the BBC she watches her smart meter at home "like a hawk".

"I'm very conscious of the cost of energy, and it is having an impact on how we function in the home as a family," she said.

"I try to cook using the oven a lot less than I used to. We haven't got the heating on yet in our household. We're just like 'wear more layers'. We've got electric blankets on the sofa."

Heating bills, chocolate and cat food: What the inflation figures mean for you

Shadow chancellor, Andrew Griffith, said four successive rises in the inflation rate was down to the UK's "soaring energy costs" and Labour's decision to "hike taxes and employment costs".

Grant Fitzner, chief economist at the ONS, said fuel prices were "once again a major contributor" to the increase in the inflation rate in October.

Rising energy prices can lead to more expensive food and staples for consumers if businesses pass on higher costs.

Earlier this month, the Bank of England said there had been "limited evidence" of this, with some firms absorbing cost increases and accepting lower profit margins.

But Fitzner said on Wednesday: "Annual costs of both raw materials and goods leaving factories continue to climb, driven by higher crude oil and petrol prices, respectively."

Petrol prices rose by 5.2p per litre between September and October, the ONS said, with the average price last month hitting 174.7p per litre - the highest since August 2022.

Diesel prices, meanwhile, reached the highest price since records began 30 years ago, averaging 199.5p per litre in October.

But the ONS said price inflation in restaurants and cafes had slowed last month, and that prices had fallen on average for hotels.

And food price inflation rose by 0.9%, the slowest pace in five years.

Core inflation, which strips out energy and food costs, and services inflation were little changed on last month. The figure is closely watched by the Bank of England when setting interest rates.

It could mean the Bank opts to hold off on a further rate rise in December, though policymakers believe inflation could peak at 4.9% in the middle of next year and not return to 2% until 2028.

In an "adverse scenario", where the US-Iran war could escalate further and send oil prices higher, inflation could reach 6.2% in 2027, it has suggested.

A spokesperson for the Bank said it decided to raise rates two weeks ago to "limit how much higher energy costs affect other prices, and will continue to set interest rates to make sure inflation returns to the 2% target and stays there".

Additional reporting by Raphael Sheridan, economics producer

#121. Lowe's posts strong online sales, raises outlook despite slow housing market

Lowe's on Wednesday posted strong quarterly results and raised its full-year outlook as the company said it saw strength among professional shoppers.

The company said comparable sales grew 1.8% for the fiscal third quarter, driven by a 17.4% increase in online sales and "solid" performance in pro and home services sales. Net sales rose to $22.76 billion.

Because of the strength, Lowe's now said it expects full-year sales of roughly $92.5 billion, up from a previous projection of $92 billion. It also expects comparable sales to be up 0.5%, compared with previous guidance of flat.

CEO Marvin Ellison told CNBC that the company is focused on growing despite economic headwinds and a sluggish housing market. He said Lowe's posted its sixth straight quarterly comparable sales increase and saw particular strength in appliances, which saw its largest revenue growth in five years.

"We believe irrespective of the macro environment we're in, we're going to take share because there's a lot of share out there that we can go and get, and we're going to be aggressive to go and get that share," Ellison told CNBC.

Lowe's also reported a pretax benefit of approximately $250 million from tariff refunds during the quarter, which contributed a 34-cent boost to earnings per share. Ellison said the company plans to use those funds to enhance the customer experience and bring value to shoppers.

He added that do-it-yourself customers and pro shoppers are not investing in major renovation projects but are instead spending on repair and maintenance projects.

"We are giving those pros what they need," he said. "We're able to improve our fulfillment capabilities and our pro loyalty program."

Here's how the company performed in its fiscal third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

For the quarter ended Oct. 30, Lowe's reported net income of $1.67 billion, or $2.98 per share, compared with $1.62 billion, or $2.88 per share, the year prior.

The home improvement retailer has previously said it's seeing "pressure" on spending on projects, especially as consumers are stretched thin by inflation and macroeconomic uncertainty. The company is also battling a slower housing market and higher mortgage rates.

Ellison previously told CNBC he expects consumers to remain cautious through the end of the year.

Lowe's executives said on a call with analysts Wednesday that the company is continuing to battle high fuel costs, which they said accelerated through the third quarter. CFO Brandon Sink said diesel prices are currently around $6 per gallon, up about 15% overall compared to what the company saw in the second quarter.

"So we're trying to continue to manage that, balance that, and we're reflecting that into our Q4 guide," Sink said.

Ellison told CNBC the company remains optimistic about consumer spending this holiday season and that November sales are in line with expectations so far.

Lowe's competitor Home Depot said earlier this week that it's seeing resilience among professional shoppers and reported comparable sales growth.

#119. Gilts v kilts: What is happening with Scottish government bonds?

The Scottish government is facing questions about its plan to issue its first ever bonds.

The move would allow ministers to borrow money from investors who in return receive regular interest payments.

The aim is to raise about Â£1.5bn by the end of the parliamentary term, with the funds to be used on long-term projects such as building hospitals or maintaining roads.

Named "kilts", a nod to UK government "gilts", the scheme has provoked much debate.

This week, MSPs on Holyrood's finance committee heard about the potential risks and rewards.

A bond is a financial tool used by a government to borrow money. They are sold with a promise for the buyer, or investor, to be paid back at the end of an agreed term.

The government will also make regular payments to the investor.

In the UK, a government bond is called a gilt. They are typically bought by financial institutions such as pension funds, insurance companies and commercial banks.

As a play on this term, the Scottish bonds have been nicknamed "kilts".

The Scottish government has said it expects the bonds to be issued within this financial year, subject to market conditions.

Former First Minister Humza Yousaf commissioned initial work in 2023 after advisers in the Scottish government's Investor Panel recommended making bonds available to the market as a means of raising Scotland's profile and attracting investment.

In November last year, the government announced that it had received an identical credit rating to the UK's score.

This was based on "prudent fiscal management" and the country's economic stability - though ministers were warned this could change if Scotland moved towards independence.

With market volatility due to conflicts in Ukraine and the Middle East, some have questioned whether now is a good time to launch bonds.

But at Holyrood, David McMillan, professor in finance and accounting at the University of Stirling, told MSPs: "You can't wait for a perfect time because there's going to be no perfect time."

The government wants to raise Â£1.5bn through bonds by 2031, although that would only be a fraction of its near Â£70bn budget.

About four-fifths of the Scottish government's budget is used on resource spending, which goes towards day-to-day costs such as funding the NHS, benefits payments and paying salaries.

A smaller portion is made up of capital spending, typically used on long-term physical assets such as buildings and bridges, or maintaining roads or paths.

The Scottish government is unable to borrow for resource spending other than to correct forecasting errors.

However, for capital spending, it can borrow as much as Â£450m a year at 2023-24 levels up to a total cap of Â£3bn - to be uprated with inflation.

Adjusted for inflation, the government could borrow about Â£491m in the current financial year, with a total limit of about Â£3.3bn.

Economists have noted that Scottish bonds would be a more costly way of borrowing than existing routes provided by the UK government through the National Loans Fund.

It has been estimated, external that interest rates on "kilts" would be 0.3 percentage points above gilts, plus an additional Â£2m per bond in administrative costs.

The Scottish government has not released costings, citing market sensitivities, but has insisted, external issuing bonds would represent value for money relative to the costs of borrowing through the UK scheme.

Ministers argue that issuing bonds would be a good opportunity to market the country to investors, and would be helpful if Scotland became independent.

Dr Ben Cormier, a senior lecturer at the University of Strathclyde's department of government and public policy, told Holyrood's finance committee that while the Scottish bonds could cost marginally more than the UK scheme, they would "bring flexibility to public finances and debt management".

McMillan agreed with the plans "in principle", but warned they would lose "credibility" with investors if limited to five years.

Cormier warned that independence was the one thing "that could materially change the nature of risk associated with Scottish bonds".

Conservative MSP Liam Kerr said bonds could be more expensive than borrowing through the National Loans Fund and voiced concerns about a lack of transparency in the programme.

McMillan agreed there should be "full information" available, saying: "You've got to have a clear, full and frank conversation with the public."

Scottish government aiming to issue first bonds in 2026-27
